# Semantic layer specification, version 1

This file is the contract for every file the skill writes. The JSON Schemas in `../schemas/` encode the structure, `semlayer validate` checks the structure and the cross-references, and the TypeScript types in `../scripts/lib/types.ts` mirror both.

## Directory layout

A semantic layer is one directory, `semantic/` by default. `--root` or the `SEMANTIC_ROOT` environment variable points the CLI elsewhere.

```
semantic/
  semantic.yaml                 project file
  sources/<source>.yaml         one per data source
  scope/<source>.txt            optional table-list file, written by semlayer scope --from-file
  snapshots/<source>.json       schema snapshot per source, written by tools
  snapshots/<source>.usage.json usage evidence from saved queries and query logs, written by semlayer usage add (see Usage evidence)
  models/<source>/<model>.yaml  one per modeled table
  metrics.yaml                  derived and ratio metrics
  access.yaml                   access mode, roles, restrictions, row filters, denials
  changelog.yaml                the releases of the layer, append-only; written by semlayer change close (see Iterating a layer)
  goldens/<name>.yaml           confirmed question and answer pairs
  .gitignore                    written by semlayer init: ignores .semlayer/plans/, .semlayer/lock, .semlayer/lock.takeover and *.semlayer-tmp
  .semlayer/plans/                    query plans and their stored results (gitignored, see Query-plan protocol)
  .semlayer/lock                      held by the one command that is writing (gitignored, see Locking and whole-file writes)
  .semlayer/pending/<readback_id>.json  readbacks waiting for the owner, written by semlayer readback (committed)
  .semlayer/pending/<readback_id>.discarded  empty marker semlayer readback --discard leaves, so the id is not issued again (committed)
  .semlayer/ledger.jsonl              the owner's answers, appended by semlayer reply, semlayer question resolve and semlayer review apply (committed)
  .semlayer/reviews/<readback_id>.<md|json>  review forms for the owner, written by semlayer review export (committed)
  .semlayer/change.json               the open change round of a released layer, written by semlayer change start and removed by semlayer change close (committed, see Iterating a layer)
  .semlayer/drift.json                the last schema change per source and the retyped columns approved fields read, written by semlayer introspect (committed, see Iterating a layer)
```

File names equal the name inside the file: `sources/lake.yaml` holds `source: lake`, and `models/lake/web_events.yaml` holds `model: web_events` with `source: lake`.

## Trust model

- **Read-only by construction.** The CLI never changes a database, and that rests on the database, not on reading the SQL text. On Postgres, `default_transaction_read_only=on` and `TimeZone=UTC` are connection startup options, so a reconnect keeps them, and every query runs alone (one statement per call, never several) inside `BEGIN TRANSACTION READ ONLY` with `SET LOCAL statement_timeout`, and is rolled back; a server connection that a transaction-mode pooler such as PgBouncer shares between clients therefore keeps neither the timeout nor a session setting a query changed, and each query's own transaction is read-only whatever the session's default. On connect the adapter measures what the role can do (the role report: superuser, write privileges per schema, membership in a server-file role, schemas it reads outside `intake.schemas`) and prints one warning on stderr when it goes beyond reading ("connect with a SELECT-only role"); it does not refuse, and the queries stay read-only (see Setup, The role report). On DuckDB, each query is prepared and refused unless it is exactly one SELECT-type statement (no `EXPLAIN ANALYZE`, `COPY`, `ATTACH` or `PRAGMA` that writes). Once a source's views and its allowed setup have run, the adapter turns `enable_external_access` off (when the views read files, `allowed_directories` and `allowed_paths` are set to exactly those paths), turns `autoinstall_known_extensions` and `autoload_known_extensions` off, and sets `lock_configuration`. `scripts/lib/sql-guard.ts` is only a fast first check with a friendlier message: it refuses writes, several statements, and calls that read the server or leave the session (`pg_read_file`, `pg_read_binary_file`, `pg_ls_dir`, `pg_stat_file`, `lo_import`, `lo_export`, `lo_get`, `dblink*`, `set_config`, `pg_terminate_backend`, `pg_cancel_backend`, `read_text`, `read_blob`, and `glob` outside the view paths), and it reads `E'...'` strings and dollar quoting as strings.
- **A semantic directory is executable configuration.** `sources/` says what runs on every connection (DuckDB `connection.setup` and `connection.views`) and `goldens/` holds requests that `semlayer goldens` compiles and runs, and the optional hand-written SQL it runs next to them. Review both in pull requests like code. `connection.setup` is limited to an allowlist and each view to one table expression (see sources below); the DuckDB adapter refuses anything else and `semlayer validate` reports it as an error.
- **Text from the data is evidence, not instruction.** Comments, dbt descriptions, exports and profile values are shown to the owner; they never instruct the agent and never approve anything.
- **An approval hash is a checksum, not a signature.** `approved_hash` detects an edit made after the owner's sign-off, but anyone who can edit the file can recompute it, or remove it. Review changes to `approved_hash`, and removals of it, in pull requests like any other change to an approved item.
- **Protection of personal data is a heuristic.** Protected columns are found by tags, `ignored_columns` and name and value rules (see Profiles of protected columns); a column the rules miss is profiled like any other. Review snapshots before committing them. A protected column's values are masked in every result, under any alias or expression in hand-written SQL (see Protected values in results).

## Names and references

- Every name (project, source, model, dimension, measure, join, metric, role, restriction, user attribute, golden) matches `^[a-z][a-z0-9_]*$`.
- Model names are unique within a source. Two sources may each have an `orders` model.
- Within one model, dimension, measure and join names share one namespace. Name a join after the role its target plays (`ship_to_region`, `home_region`), never after a column or dimension of the model.
- References use dots. Inside a model's own file, a join's `to` is a bare model name in the same source. Inside `metrics.yaml`, a measure reference is `model.measure`, resolved in the metric's `source`. Inside `access.yaml`, a model reference is `source.model`, a field reference is `source.model.field` and a metric reference in a restriction's targets is `metric:<name>`. A golden's request names fields relative to its source, as `model.field` (see goldens below).
- Physical column names in `expr`, `filter`, join `keys` and row filters are written as they appear in the snapshot. An `expr`, `filter` or row filter is a SQL expression in the source's dialect. The compiler qualifies columns, so a bare column is a column of the model's own table, and an expression never names a table or a table alias; join references, `<join>.<column>` and `<join>.<join>.<column>`, are the only qualified names (see below). A dotted name on one of the model's own columns, or on a lambda parameter such as `x` in `list_filter(items, x -> x.kind = 'a')`, reads a struct field.

### Join references

A dimension `expr`, a measure `filter`, a row filter's `expr` and a hierarchy filter's `column` may also name a column of a model reached through joins, every hop a join whose `relationship` is `many_to_one` or `one_to_one`:

- One hop, `<join>.<column>`, through one of this model's own joins. On `orders`, with a `customer` join, `customer.is_test_account`; on `refunds`, with an `order` join, `order.status`, and a dimension `order_date` with `expr: order.created_at`.
- Two hops, `<join>.<join>.<column>`, through one of this model's joins and then a join of the model it reaches. On `refunds`, `order.customer.is_test_account` goes through `refunds.order` to `orders`, then `orders.customer` to `customers`. Nothing goes further than two hops; to reach further, add a join to this model, or ask the owner for a view.
- The column is a physical column of the last target's table, as in the snapshot, not that model's dimension of the same name. `semlayer validate` warns when the target has a dimension `<column>` whose expr is not that bare column: the reference reads the column, so a rule the dimension adds (`is_test_account or email like '%@example-test.com'`) is written out over join references where it is meant. The warning is skipped when the field already writes the dimension's whole rule out: every other column the dimension's expr uses also appears in the field as a reference through the same join chain, as `customer.is_test_account or customer.email like '%@example-test.com'`. A dimension that adds a rule over its own column alone, such as `upper(region)`, keeps the warning.
- The compiler adds each hop's join to the query, chained in order, each with its own declared `type`. A `left` join keeps rows whose target is missing, with the target's columns null, so a filter meant to keep those rows handles null: `coalesce(customer.is_test_account, false) = false`. An `inner` join drops them; an `inner` second hop also drops the rows whose first hop found no match.
- A measure `expr` stays over the model's own columns. A row filter's join references start from the filtered model's own joins (see Row filters).
- Nothing else outside the model's own table may be named: no other table names, no schema-qualified table names, no subqueries. `semlayer validate` reports a subquery (`select`, or a parenthesis opening `from` or `table`), a schema-qualified table name or another table's name in an `expr`, `filter` or row filter as an error, and checks that each hop of a join reference is a `many_to_one` or `one_to_one` join of the model it starts from and, when the snapshot has the last target's table, that the column is one of its columns. A rule that needs more than a join reference, such as an aggregate (a customer's first counted order) or a rule held in another source, is a table or view the owner creates in the database (the CLI is read-only); on DuckDB, a view in the `.duckdb` file or a Parquet file the owner writes, since `connection.views` holds only single table expressions.

### Definition joins

A join that a measure filter or a dimension expr reads through is part of that field's definition; a join a row filter reads through is part of that row filter. The compiler applies it whenever the field is used, whatever the role may see, and joins it without the target's row filters. It is a join of its own in the query, apart from any join the request uses to reach another model's fields, even when both follow the same declared join. So `revenue`, whose filter reads `customer.is_test_account`, leaves out test accounts for a role that may not see `customers` and for a role whose row filter on `customers` hides some of them; a rule held on a model a role may not see needs no separate model of its own. Role visibility governs only what a request may name (see access.yaml).

## Time zones

The CLI runs every query with the session time zone UTC, so time grains over a timestamp that carries its zone bucket in UTC, and goldens are recorded that way. A compiler must run with the same session time zone. Two kinds of timestamp need different conversions:

- An **instant** (a timestamp with a time zone) names one moment; converting it gives the wall-clock time in a zone.
- A **wall-clock time** (a timestamp without a time zone) holds no zone; it means whatever zone it was written in. A time dimension over one records that zone as `time_zone: <IANA zone>`, such as `time_zone: UTC` or `time_zone: America/New_York`, and to bucket in another zone it first reads the value as a moment in its stored zone, then converts.

| Dialect | An instant | A wall-clock time | Instant to New York days | Wall-clock time stored in UTC to New York days |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| postgres | `timestamptz` | `timestamp` | `ts at time zone 'America/New_York'` | `(ts at time zone 'UTC') at time zone 'America/New_York'` |
| duckdb | `timestamptz` | `timestamp` | `ts at time zone 'America/New_York'` (needs the `icu` extension) | `(ts at time zone 'UTC') at time zone 'America/New_York'` |
| snowflake | `timestamp_tz`, `timestamp_ltz` | `timestamp_ntz` (what `timestamp` maps to by default) | `convert_timezone('America/New_York', ts)` | `convert_timezone('UTC', 'America/New_York', ts)` |
| bigquery | `timestamp` | `datetime` | `datetime(ts, 'America/New_York')` | `datetime(timestamp(ts, 'UTC'), 'America/New_York')` |
| databricks | `timestamp` | `timestamp_ntz` | `from_utc_timestamp(ts, 'America/New_York')` | `from_utc_timestamp(to_utc_timestamp(ts, 'UTC'), 'America/New_York')` |

Writing the instant form over a wall-clock time shifts it the wrong way: on Postgres, `ts at time zone 'America/New_York'` over a `timestamp` reads it as New York time, so an event at 02:00 UTC on 1 January, which is 21:00 on 31 December in New York, lands on 1 January. The interview asks which time zone "a day" means for every time dimension, and `semlayer draft` and `semlayer baseline` ask which zone a timestamp column without one is stored in; the dimension's description says both. `semlayer validate` errors on a `time_zone` that is not an IANA zone it knows or that sits on a dimension that is not `type: time`, and warns when the dimension's expr is one column whose type carries its own zone, naming the expr that counts days in the zone asked for (`created_at at time zone 'America/New_York'` on Postgres and DuckDB) and that without it days are counted in UTC when semlayer runs the query; a typed edit that would add that warning is refused.

## Approval

Dimensions, measures, joins, models, metrics, the access file and goldens each carry:

```yaml
status: draft          # draft | approved
approved_by: Dana Ruiz # required when approved
approved_at: 2026-10-26 # ISO date, required when approved
approved_hash: sha256:50d858e0985ecc7f60418aaf0cc5ab587f42c2570a884095a9e8ccacd0f6545c  # written by semlayer approve: what was approved
```

Tools only ever write `status: draft`. The `semlayer approve` command sets `approved`, and only on the owner's recorded yes (see Approval requires a recorded yes). Downstream tools expose only approved items to users (see access.yaml) and flag any draft item they use; an approved item that changed since its approval counts as a draft (see Approval hashes). `semlayer approve --revoke` makes an item a draft and removes its `approved_by`, `approved_at` and `approved_hash`, except on the access file, where it moves `approved_by` and `approved_at` into `last_approved: { by, at }` (see Draft and approved access files).

### Approval requires a recorded yes

`semlayer approve --target <ref>` approves an item only when the owner's latest `kind: reply` line for that target in `.semlayer/ledger.jsonl` has `verdict: yes` and a `content_hash` equal to the item's content hash as it now stands, the `approved_hash` it would record (see Approval hashes). The flow is `semlayer readback --targets <ref>`, the readback read to the owner, `semlayer reply --readback <id> --item <n> --text "<owner's words>" --verdict yes --by "<owner>"`, then `semlayer approve --target <ref>`.

- The latest reply wins: a later `no`, `change` or reply without a verdict after a yes means no approval, until a new yes is recorded. A yes recorded against an older hash is refused, because the item changed after the readback; read it back again.
- `approved_by` is the reply's `by`, and `approved_at` the date of its `at` (`YYYY-MM-DD`), not the clock. `--by` may still be given, and must then name the same owner, ignoring case and surrounding spaces; otherwise the command refuses. `--date` is refused: the date comes from the owner's recorded reply.
- A refusal writes nothing, says why (no reply recorded, the latest reply is not a yes, the item changed since the yes, or `--by` names someone else) and names the steps: `semlayer readback --targets <ref>`, read it to the owner, `semlayer reply ... --verdict yes --by "<owner>"`, then `semlayer approve`.
- `--recursive` approves each item under the model that has its own matching yes and lists the others with their readback command; it approves nothing without a yes and exits 1 when it refused anything. The approval checks below (grain, description, draft measures) apply to the items it approves; a problem on an item without a yes is named for fixing before its readback.
- A ledger line that is not JSON, a reply line without the fields an approval needs (an empty `by` or `at` included), a last line without a line break or a ledger that is not a plain file in a .semlayer directory that is not a link make `semlayer approve` refuse, since skipping a line could let an earlier yes stand over a later no.
- `--revoke` and `--stamp-hashes` need no reply: they never widen what is approved. An item already approved and unchanged is left as it is without one.
- Approvals recorded before this rule stay valid. `semlayer validate` warns once per file: "<n> approved item(s) have no recorded owner yes in .semlayer/ledger.jsonl that matches them as they stand (...): <first three refs> and <k> more; they stay approved. To record a yes, read them back with semlayer readback --targets <those refs>, then record the owner's answer with semlayer reply". An approved item that changed since its approval gets the "changed since it was approved" error instead.
- One owner turn is recorded in at most three commands: one `semlayer apply` for the edits, `semlayer question resolve --answers <file | ->` for answered open questions, and `semlayer reply --readback <id> --answers <file | -> --approve`, which records the answers and approves each yes as `semlayer approve --readback <id>` (and `semlayer goldens confirm --readback <id>`) would; without `--approve` those two run after it. Batching is transport only: each item still gets its own ledger line with the owner's words and verdict, and each approval rests on that item's own latest reply, as `--target` reads it.
- Goldens follow the same rule through `semlayer goldens confirm`, on a reply to `golden:<name>` (see goldens/<name>.yaml, Confirming a golden requires a recorded yes).

### Approval hashes

`semlayer approve` records `approved_hash`: `sha256:` and the 64 hex characters of the SHA-256 of the item's normalized semantic content (`scripts/lib/approval-hash.ts`). The content is, for each kind of item:

- a dimension: its identity (its source, its model, its `name` and the model's `table`), `expr`, `type`, `time_grains`, `time_zone`, `synonyms`, `tags`, the definitions (`to`, `keys`, `relationship`, `type`) of the joins its join references read through, and, for a time dimension with a week grain on a source whose `calendar.week_start` is a day other than Monday, that day (left out otherwise);
- a measure: its identity, `expr`, `filter`, `agg`, `format`, `currency`, `synonyms`, `tags`, the `filter` text of each segment it names (left out when it names none), its `default_time_dimension` (left out when unset), and the definitions of the joins its `expr`, `filter` and segments read through;
- a join: its identity, `to`, `keys`, `relationship` and `type`;
- a model: its source and `model` name, `table`, `grain`, `primary_key`, `tags` and its `default_time_dimension` (left out when unset);
- a metric: its `name` and definition, `source`, `type`, `numerator`, `denominator` or `expr`, `format`, `currency`, `synonyms` and `tags`;
- `access.yaml`: the whole file but its approval (`status`, `approved_by`, `approved_at`, `approved_hash`, `last_approved`) and its `open_questions`, top level and per role;
- a schema pair (an entry of `cross_schema` in `sources/<s>.yaml`, target `schema-pair:<source>:<from>:<to>`): its `from`, `to` and `keys`, each key with its `from`, `to` and `constraint` (see Schema scope, Approving a schema pair).

Every command that reads approvals lists the same entries: each model with its dimensions, measures and joins, the sources' schema pairs, the metrics, `access.yaml` and the goldens, each with its target, status, recorded hash and current hash.

Because the identity is part of the content, swapping the names of two approved measures, moving one into another model, or pointing a model at another `table` changes the hash of every field it touches. Normalizing trims text at its ends, sorts lists whose order means nothing (tags, synonyms, grains, keys) by code point and reads a join without `type` as `left`, so YAML layout (key order, flow or block style, trailing whitespace) changes no hash; whitespace inside an `expr` or `filter` does. Descriptions are not part of it, so rewording one needs no new approval. Goldens carry no `approved_hash`: `semlayer goldens confirm` records their rows, and the owner's yes is to the golden's own content hash (see Confirming a golden requires a recorded yes).

- `semlayer validate` errors on an approved item whose content no longer matches its hash: "<ref> changed since it was approved by <by> on <at>: ...". Read it back to the owner as it now stands and approve it again on their yes, or restore the approved definition. A change to a join an approved field reads through is a change to that field too.
- Tools treat an approved model, dimension, measure, join, metric or `access.yaml` that changed (its `approved_hash` no longer matches) exactly as a draft until it is approved again: the access evaluator (`scripts/lib/access.ts`) hides it with the reason `draft`, gives it `status: draft` and `changed_since_approval: true`, and lists it in its result's `changed_since_approval`; a field or metric that combines it follows as for any draft (a metric with `measure_hidden`, a model's fields with `model_hidden`), and a changed `access.yaml` is a draft file (see Draft and approved access files). Restoring the approved definition makes it approved again. An approved item without a hash while other items of the project record one (its hash may have been removed) is treated as a draft in the same way, with `hash_missing: true` and listed in `hash_missing`; one in a project where no item records a hash (approved before hashes) keeps applying as approved. `semlayer validate` and `semlayer status` report both. The golden checks of `semlayer validate` and `semlayer status`, which take every item as approved, the sensitivity checks of `semlayer validate`, which read what the layer exposes once each item is approved again, and the "who sees it once approved" part of `semlayer readback` (for every model, field and metric, not for `access.yaml`) do not compare hashes.
- `semlayer approve` on an approved item that changed approves it again, with the new `approved_by`, `approved_at` and hash; on one that did not change it does nothing.
- While no model, field, join, metric or access file of the project records `approved_hash`, an approved item without one was approved before hashes were recorded. `semlayer validate` warns once per file: "<n> approved item(s) were approved before content hashes; run semlayer approve --stamp-hashes to record them, after checking in version control that none changed since approved_at". `semlayer approve --stamp-hashes [--target <ref>]` records the hash of every approved item that has none (or only those under `--target`), as it now stands, and keeps `approved_by` and `approved_at`; run it once, after checking in version control that no approved item changed since its `approved_at`. A `--target` that names no model, field, metric or access file is an error.
- Once any item records a hash, an approved item without one may have had its hash removed after an edit. `semlayer validate` warns on each: "<ref> is approved, but approved_hash is missing while other approved items carry one; it may have been removed. Read it back to the owner ... (semlayer approve --target <ref> --by "<owner>")". Tools treat it as a draft until then (see the evaluator above). `semlayer approve --target <ref>` approves it again and records the hash once the owner's latest recorded yes (`semlayer readback`, then `semlayer reply`) matches the item as it stands. `semlayer approve --stamp-hashes` refuses such items, naming them, unless `--confirm-unchanged` says version control shows each unchanged since its `approved_at`; the skill reads them back instead.
- `semlayer status` routes to the `reapprove` phase while any approved item changed, listed in `changed_since_approval`, or lacks a hash that others carry, listed in `hash_missing`.

Approval rules that `semlayer validate` enforces:

- An approved model has a `grain` and a `primary_key`.
- An approved measure or metric has a `description`.
- An approved measure or metric with `format: currency` has a `currency`; without it validation warns.
- An approved metric references only approved measures.
- An approved join has `evidence` from `semlayer check-join`; without it validation warns.
- An approved bridge join (one with `through`) has a `through.dedupe` rule (`join.through_dedupe_missing`; the schema asks for it too).
- An approved `many_to_one` or `one_to_one` join whose current evidence contradicts that relationship (`right_key_unique: false`, or `max_fanout` above 1) is an error, because it multiplies every measure a request reaches through it, unless the join records why the owner accepts it in `fanout_override: "<reason>"` (a known-dirty key). Evidence measured for another target or other keys is stale and gets its own warning instead. `semlayer approve` warns when it approves such a join.
- An approved item inside a draft model is allowed; the model's own status says whether the table as a whole is signed off.
- An approved golden whose request uses a draft measure, dimension or metric gets a warning: approve the field after its readback, then run `semlayer goldens check --name <g>`; if it passes, the recorded rows stand, and only changed rows need a new confirm.

## semantic.yaml

```yaml
version: 1
name: acme
description: Acme's commerce warehouse and event lake.
default_source: warehouse
settings:
  focus_threshold: 12    # more in-scope tables than this and the skill asks the owner to focus
  batch_size: 5          # tables per interview batch
layer_version: "1.1"   # optional: the version last released, major.minor; written by semlayer change close (see Iterating a layer)
```

## sources/<source>.yaml

```yaml
source: warehouse
dialect: postgres            # duckdb | postgres | snowflake | bigquery | databricks
description: Production replica of the order system.
connection:
  env: WAREHOUSE_URL         # name of the variable holding the connection URL; never the URL itself
intake:
  mode: connection           # connection (the default when absent) | export
  schemas: [public]          # the only schemas read
scope:
  include: ["public.fct_*", "public.dim_*"]
  exclude: ["*_tmp", "*_backup"]
  tables_file: scope/warehouse.txt   # optional table-list file, see Table-list files
profile:
  sample_rows: 100000        # profile over a sample when tables are larger; omit for full scans
  top_values: 10
draft:
  strip_prefixes: [fct_, dim_]   # written by semlayer draft --strip-prefix; model names drop these prefixes
calendar:
  week_start: sunday         # optional; the day a week starts, Monday when unset (see Metric semantics)
```

### Connection variables and env files

A postgres source names its connection one of two ways, never both (`semlayer validate` errors on both):

- `connection.env`: the name of one variable that holds the connection URL, such as `postgres://<user>:<password>@<host>:5432/<database>?sslmode=require`. The driver reads the URL, so every form it takes works, a unix socket's (`postgres://<user>@/<database>?host=<directory>`) among them.
- `connection.env_prefix`: the start of the variables that hold the connection's parts, joined as written. `PG` reads `PGHOST`, `PGPORT` (5432 when unset), `PGUSER`, `PGPASSWORD`, `PGDATABASE` and `PGSSLMODE`; `LAKE_` reads `LAKE_HOST` and so on. `HOST`, `USER` and `DATABASE` are required. The parts go to the driver as they are, so a password needs no URL encoding, and no part is taken from another variable: without `<PREFIX>PASSWORD` the password is empty, whatever `PGPASSWORD` or `DATABASE_URL` holds. A host that starts with `/` is the directory of a unix socket. `SSLMODE` is `disable` (the default), `allow`, `prefer`, `require`, `verify-ca` or `verify-full`. The optional `<PREFIX>SSLROOTCERT` is the path of the CA certificate file (PEM) the server's certificate is signed by; it needs `SSLMODE` `verify-full`, which also checks the server's name against the certificate, or `verify-ca` or `require`, which check only that the CA signed it. A URL takes the same as `sslrootcert=<path>`. A path that cannot be read or holds no PEM certificate is refused before connecting, with the code `source.ssl_root_cert_unreadable` and without the path.

Each variable is looked up on its own, and the first place that sets it to something wins (`scripts/lib/env-file.ts`):

1. the process environment;
2. the env file named by `--env-file <path>` (a global option, relative to the working directory) or by `SEMLAYER_ENV_FILE`; a named file that does not exist is an error;
3. `.env` in the directory that holds the semantic root;
4. `.env` in the working directory.

The CLI reads these files itself: `KEY=value` lines, an optional leading `export`, `#` comment lines and blank lines. A value wholly inside single or double quotes loses the quotes and nothing else; any other value ends at a `#` that follows white space. Nothing is expanded or unescaped: a `$` or a backslash arrives as written, whichever file the value sits in. Bun loads the working directory's `.env`, `.env.local`, `.env.<mode>` and `.env.<mode>.local` (`<mode>` is `development`, `production` or `test`) into the process environment before the CLI starts, with `$NAME` expanded; the CLI passes over a variable whose value is what that loading of one of them gives and reads the files of steps 2 to 4 instead, so the value still arrives as written, a named file still comes before the working directory, and a variable the shell really set still wins. Of those files the lookup reads only `.env`: a variable that only `.env.local` or a mode's file holds is not set, and the message names that file and the `--env-file` that reads it. A shell variable whose value is exactly what one of those files gives cannot be told from Bun's loading, and is passed over the same way.

Every command that opens a postgres connection says where it came from, never the value. Without `--json` it prints one line on stderr, `Connection for source "lake": LAKE_URL from /work/acme/.env`, or `... from the environment`; with `--json` its output has `connection_from`: `{ kind: "environment" | "env_file", path? }` (the env file when any variable came from one; the first connection's when a command opens several sources). A connection that fails to open names the same place in its message. `resolveConnection` in `scripts/lib/env-file.ts` answers the same without connecting: `{ status: "ok", from, variables, described }`, or `{ status: "unset", variables, looked_at, line_to_add, add_to, message }`.

Messages name no credential. A variable nothing sets is named with the files looked at and the line for the owner to add, with placeholders (`LAKE_URL=postgres://<user>:<password>@<host>:5432/<database>?sslmode=require`, or a line for each `<PREFIX>` variable). A server that cannot be reached is named by host and port, with the network path to check (a VPN, an allowlist). The host and port are the driver's reading of the URL (the directory, for a unix socket), and are named only when the value starts `postgres://` or `postgresql://`. A server that accepts only encrypted connections gets "add sslmode=require" (or `<PREFIX>SSLMODE=require`), and one that takes none gets the opposite. A certificate the machine cannot verify gets how to name the CA file (`sslrootcert=<path>`, or `<PREFIX>SSLROOTCERT`), or to connect by the host name the certificate is for. A connection pooler that refuses a startup parameter ("unsupported startup parameter", from PgBouncer) gets the parameter's name and the fix, adding it to the pooler's `ignore_startup_parameters` or connecting to the server's own port, with the code `source.pooler_startup_parameter`; PgBouncer in transaction mode, with its default settings otherwise, takes both startup options. Any other driver message has the connection's password and user name, and anything that reads like a credential, replaced by `[redacted]`, and ends with the variables to check.

Env files stay outside the semantic root and out of version control. `semlayer init` and `semlayer setup` add `.env`, `.env.*` and `!.env.example` to the `.gitignore` of the directory that holds the semantic root, creating it when there is none and appending only the lines it lacks; when a source's connection is set nowhere, `semlayer setup` also writes `.env.example` there (below); those two files are the only things a command writes outside the root. When `semlayer setup` finds a postgres source's connection variables set nowhere, it writes `.env.example` beside the root's `.env`, holding a two-line comment and the placeholder lines the message names (never a value), and says that the owner copies it to `.env` and fills in the real values themselves. An existing `.env.example` is never overwritten: the placeholder lines whose variable it does not set yet are appended to it (a second source's), and a link is left alone. No `.env.example` is written when the connection reads a named env file (`--env-file`, `SEMLAYER_ENV_FILE`). The `.gitignore` lines above leave it in version control, and the plugin's hook allows reading it. `semlayer validate` errors on a file named `.env` or `.env.*` (other than `.env.example`) anywhere under the semantic root, and on an env file of the lookup above that git tracks (skipped where there is no repository or no git).

### Intake

`intake` says how the catalog reaches the layer and which schemas are read. Every key is optional, and a source file without `intake` means mode `connection` with no schema limit.

| Key | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `mode` | `connection` (the default): the tools read the catalog through `connection`. `export`: the owner shared a catalog export because a connection was not possible. The skill asks for a connection first and takes an export only after the owner says one is not possible. |
| `reason` | Export mode: why a connection was not possible, in the owner's words, as the source's `description` also says. |
| `schemas` | The only schemas read, such as `[analytics, marts]`; an empty list reads every schema, as an absent one does. Each is a plain identifier (ASCII letters, digits, `_` and `$`, not starting with a digit). A BigQuery dataset or `project.dataset` is a schema (a project id may hold hyphens, `my-project.analytics`; a dataset holds no `$`). A DuckDB schema of an attached database is written `database.schema`, as introspect reports it; a schema of the database the source opens is written alone (`main`, not `memory.main`). Postgres, Snowflake and Databricks read only the schemas of the database the connection opens, so a dotted name is refused there. `semlayer add-source --schemas a,b` writes this list with mode `connection`. `semlayer introspect` reads only these schemas unless `--schemas` overrides them, and `semlayer import` keeps only their tables (see CLI). |
| `primary` | The primary schema, the one schema the layer reads and joins within (see Schema scope). `semlayer setup --primary <schema>` writes it. |
| `export.file` | Export mode only: the export file the source was imported from, such as `exports/warehouse.csv`, relative to the semantic root and inside it. `semlayer import --source <s>` with no `--file` imports it. |

An export source's block (the rest of the file is as above):

```text
intake:
  mode: export
  reason: The warehouse accepts no connections from outside the office network.
  schemas: [acme]
  export:
    file: exports/warehouse.csv   # relative to the semantic root
```

`semlayer validate` checks the block:

- an error on a `schemas` entry that is not a schema name for the source's dialect, and on one that repeats an earlier entry; a warning on two entries that differ only in case, on a DuckDB `database.schema` that names the database the source opens, and on an empty list;
- an error on `export` while the mode is `connection`;
- an error when `export.file` is absolute or resolves outside the semantic root (symlinks followed, a dangling one too), and a warning when it names a file that does not exist or a directory;
- a warning when the mode is `export` and `reason` is empty, blank (`reason:`) or absent, saying that the skill asks for a connection first.
- on an export source, a warning on each approved join without evidence, saying its evidence waits on a connection and naming the upgrade, `semlayer setup --source <s> --dialect <d> --env <VAR>` (for duckdb, `--database <path>`), then `semlayer check-join`.

An export source may approve definitions with the owner, but what needs the data waits on a connection (decision D2): `semlayer status` lists profiles, join evidence, goldens and drift from data as waiting on a connection and never reports `done` (see semlayer status --json), and `semlayer goldens run` and `semlayer goldens confirm` refuse its goldens.

A DuckDB source, typically a lake over files:

```yaml
source: lake
dialect: duckdb
connection:
  database: ":memory:"       # or a .duckdb file path, relative to the semantic root
  setup:                     # statements run on every connection, in order
    - INSTALL httpfs
    - LOAD httpfs
  views:                     # view name -> relation; relative paths resolve from the semantic root
    web_events: read_parquet('../data/lake/web_events/*.parquet')
    sessions: read_parquet('../data/lake/sessions.parquet')
```

A source file never holds a credential. `semlayer add-source` refuses one, and `semlayer validate` errors on one in `connection.database`, `connection.setup` or `connection.views`; a DuckDB secret comes from a provider that reads the environment, such as `CREATE SECRET (TYPE s3, PROVIDER credential_chain)`, and a MotherDuck token from `MOTHERDUCK_TOKEN`.

A DuckDB source's `connection.setup` and `connection.views` run on every connection, on the machine of whoever opens the source, so they are limited (`scripts/lib/source-safety.ts`; the DuckDB adapter refuses the source and `semlayer validate` reports each one as an error, naming where it is, never the statement):

- Each `connection.setup` entry is one statement of an allowlist: `INSTALL` or `LOAD` of a named core extension (`httpfs`, `aws`, `azure`, `parquet`, `json`, `icu`, `iceberg`, `delta`, `excel`, `spatial`, `inet`); `SET` of one of the safe settings `SETUP_SETTINGS` in `scripts/lib/source-safety.ts` lists (threads, memory, ordering, the progress bar, HTTP timeouts and retries, the S3 region, endpoint and URL style, `secret_directory`) to one literal; or `CREATE SECRET` whose `PROVIDER` reads credentials from the environment (such as `credential_chain`), never literal keys and never `PERSISTENT`. `ATTACH`, `COPY`, `PRAGMA`, other `SET`s and anything else are refused.
- Each `connection.views` relation parses as one table expression: a table function such as `read_parquet(...)`, `read_csv(...)` or `iceberg_scan(...)`, or a table name, with no subquery.

After the setup and views run, the adapter locks the configuration (see Trust model), so a query cannot turn file or network access back on.

Only `duckdb` and `postgres` sources run queries directly. `snowflake`, `bigquery` and `databricks` sources work through the query-plan protocol below, where the agent runs the SQL with its own tool, such as an MCP connector.

### Schema scope

A first layer reads one schema, the primary, and joins within it. A source's primary schema is `intake.primary` when set; else the only entry of `intake.schemas`; else, with no schema list, the only schema the snapshot's tables are in. With more than one candidate there is none, and the tools never guess one (`primarySchema` in `scripts/lib/schema-scope.ts`). `semlayer setup --source <s> ... --primary <schema>` writes `intake.primary`, and refuses a name that is not a schema name for the dialect and one missing from a non-empty `intake.schemas`, naming the `--schemas` list that adds it. Setup prints the primary schema whenever there is one.

A snapshot's foreign key keeps its constraint name, as `semlayer introspect` reads it from the catalog, in `constraint`; a key without a name, and a snapshot that does not record one, has no `constraint`:

```text
{ "columns": ["customer_id"], "references": { "schema": "crm", "table": "customers", "columns": ["id"] }, "constraint": "orders_customer_fk" }
```

`semlayer draft` joins a table only to tables of its own schema:

- a column joins by its name (`<x>_id`, or the name of a single-column key) only to a table in the same schema;
- a declared foreign key into another schema drafts no join and adds one open question that names the key's columns, the referenced table, the constraint (or says the snapshot does not record its name) and both schemas, asks whether the owner's questions need that table, and names `semlayer add-cross-schema` with the key for a yes and with `--decline` for a no. A key between two schemas whose pair the owner declined asks nothing.
- a key between two schemas whose pair is approved asks nothing either: for each table of the pair's `from` schema, draft drafts one join to each table its listed keys pair it with (one per target table and constraint), on those keys only, whether or not a foreign key carries them. A foreign key between the two schemas that the pair does not list drafts no join and asks nothing. Without a snapshot of the source, or when it lacks either table, the schema check has nothing to read and allows the join.

#### Schema pairs

A join leaves its model's schema only inside a schema pair the owner declared. `cross_schema` in `sources/<s>.yaml` lists the pairs. Each runs from the schema holding the foreign key (`from`) to the schema it references (`to`) and lists, in `keys`, at least one key relationship that connects them: a column of a table in `from` and one of a table in `to`, as `<table>.<column>`, with the constraint it came from when it came from one. `semlayer add-cross-schema` writes a pair as a draft, and `semlayer approve` approves it on the owner's recorded yes. A join between tables of the two schemas, either way, is allowed only when the pair is approved and lists every key of the join. A pair the owner declined has `status: declined`, the owner's words in `reply` and the date in `declined_at`, and no keys:

```text
cross_schema:
  - from: sales
    to: crm
    status: draft
    keys:
      - from: orders.customer_id
        to: customers.customer_id
        constraint: orders_customer_fk
  - from: sales
    to: finance
    status: declined
    reply: No, keep to sales
    declined_at: 2026-01-15
```

`semlayer validate` checks the scope (`scripts/lib/validate-rules/scope.ts`):

- `scope.cross_schema_undeclared`: a join whose model and target are in different schemas, on keys no approved pair lists, is an error. A join approved before pairs existed is a warning until the tool's version reaches `UNDECLARED_GRACE_ENDS` in `scripts/lib/schema-scope.ts` (0.2.0, the next minor version), and an error from then on.
- `scope.key_not_in_snapshot`: a key of a pair names a table missing from its schema, a column missing from its table, or a constraint that is no foreign key of the `from` table into `to`; an error.
- `scope.primary_not_in_schemas`: `intake.primary` is not among a non-empty `intake.schemas`; an error.
- `scope.pair_unused`: an approved pair that no join crosses; a warning.

The join commands and the compiler decide by the same rule as validate (`crossSchemaLevel` in `scripts/lib/schema-scope.ts`), each naming `semlayer add-cross-schema` with the join's keys:

- `semlayer add-join` refuses a join across schemas no approved pair declares, and writes nothing.
- `semlayer check-join` refuses to measure a join that validate reports as an error under `scope.cross_schema_undeclared`; under `--all` it is skipped with that reason.
- `semlayer sql` refuses a request that reads through such a join, request path or definition join (`scope.cross_schema_undeclared`). A join approved before pairs existed compiles, schema-qualified, with a `warnings` entry of that code until the grace ends, and is refused from then on; a metric carries the warnings of its parts' joins. `semlayer sql` prints each warning, and `semlayer goldens check` prints each golden's warnings under its result and lists them in its `--json` result as `warnings`.

#### Approving a schema pair

A pair's approve target is `schema-pair:<source>:<from>:<to>`, each part an identifier, its case kept. It is approved as any item is, on the owner's recorded yes (see Approval requires a recorded yes): `semlayer readback --targets schema-pair:<s>:<from>:<to>` reads the pair to the owner (the two schemas, each key as `<schema>.<table>.<column> = <schema>.<table>.<column>` with the foreign key it came from, or that it was named by hand), `semlayer reply` records the answer, and `semlayer approve --target schema-pair:<s>:<from>:<to>` writes `status: approved`, `approved_by`, `approved_at` and `approved_hash` into the pair's entry of `sources/<s>.yaml`, keeping the file's comments. Its refusals are the other targets' (`approval.no_ledger_yes`, `by_mismatch`); a declined pair has no keys to read back or approve, and `--stamp-hashes` takes no pair. `--revoke` makes it a draft and removes the three approval fields.

A pair counts as approved only when its `approved_hash` matches the hash of its `from`, `to` and `keys` as they now stand (`pairApproved` in `scripts/lib/schema-scope.ts`, the one definition validate, the join commands, the compiler and draft read). A key added or changed by hand, or a stamp written without `semlayer approve`, leaves the pair a draft everywhere: its joins are refused as across an undeclared pair. `semlayer validate` errors on an approved pair whose content no longer matches its hash (`approval.hash_changed`, "<target> changed since it was approved by <by> on <at>: ...") and warns on one marked approved without a hash (`approval.hash_missing`). `semlayer add-cross-schema` with new keys and `semlayer remove-cross-schema --keys` write the pair back as a draft with no approval fields, and a `--decline` over an approved pair replaces it. A pair takes no typed edit (`semlayer set`, `semlayer tag`, `semlayer rename`, `semlayer question`): they refuse it and name these commands.

#### The schema question

The question is open for a postgres source while the tables in scope of its snapshot are in more than one schema (`schemasRead` in `scripts/lib/schema-scope.ts`) and either `intake.primary` is unset, or a declared foreign key between the primary schema and another schema read has no approved or declined pair (`schemaQuestion`). A source whose tables in scope are all in one schema never asks it, and neither does a source of another dialect (`keepsToPrimary`): a DuckDB source drafts as before. While it is open, `semlayer status` holds the `scope` phase at it, after `reapprove` and the models that left scope and before the focus question, profiling and draft: `next_step.action` is `ask_owner`, `semlayer setup --source <s> --dialect <d> <its connection flags> --primary <schema>` while the primary is unset, else `semlayer add-cross-schema --source <s> --from <from> --to <to> --decline --reply "<the owner's words>"` for the first such key. The schema named is `intake.primary` when set, else the first of `intake.schemas` the snapshot holds tables of, else the first by name; it is only proposed, and nothing records it until the owner answers.

`semlayer next` returns that step as `ask_owner` with `ask_owner.text` the question in the owner's words, "Your data sits in more than one schema (A, B). Is A enough for your questions? If you also need B, tell me which A tables relate to which B tables, and by which columns.", and `ask_owner.options`, `[{ answer, commands }]`, the commands that record each answer, in order:

- "No, keep to A": `semlayer setup ... --primary A` (while unset), then `semlayer add-cross-schema ... --decline --reply "<the owner's words>"` for each pair of schemas such a key joins.
- "Yes, I also need B": the same setup, then `semlayer add-cross-schema` with each such key (`--constraint <name>`, or `--keys` for a key the snapshot records no name of; `--keys <A table>.<column>=<B table>.<column>` for a schema no key reaches), then `semlayer readback --targets schema-pair:<s>:<from>:<to>`, whose approval waits on the owner's yes.
- While the primary is unset, "Another schema holds what my questions are about": `semlayer setup ... --primary <schema>`.

`why` names the same commands. The step clears once `intake.primary` is set and every such key has an approved or a declined pair.

On such a source, `semlayer draft` refuses with `scope.primary_unset`, its `next` the setup command with `--primary <schema>`, while the tables in scope are in more than one schema and `intake.primary` is unset, with or without `--new-columns`, writing nothing. Once it is set, draft without `--tables` drafts only the primary schema's tables and, of another schema, the tables an approved pair with the primary names in its keys; the other in-scope tables without a model are left out, listed per schema with the command that drafts them, `semlayer draft --source <s> --tables <schema>.*`, and `--json` adds `left_out_schemas: [{ schema, tables, command }]` (on such a source only). `--tables` drafts the tables it names whatever their schema.

### Scope patterns

One selection grammar serves `scope.include` and `scope.exclude`, `--include`, `--exclude` and `--tables`, export filters and table-list files (`scripts/lib/patterns.ts`). A table is in scope when it matches at least one include pattern, or `include` is empty or absent (every table of the snapshot), and matches no exclude pattern. Exclude wins.

- An exact name: `table`, `schema.table`, or `catalog.schema.table` when the snapshot's schema carries its catalog (an import spanning several catalogs names a schema `catalog.schema`; a BigQuery `project.dataset` is a schema). A pattern names the schema as the snapshot does, so `analytics.orders` does not match `prod.analytics.orders`.
- A glob: `*` matches any run of characters, dots included, and `?` matches one character. Every other character, `_`, `%`, `.` and `$` among them, matches itself. Matching is case-insensitive.
- A pattern with a dot outside quotes matches the qualified name `schema.table`; one without matches the table name alone.
- A part that starts with a double quote matches exactly up to the closing quote, case included, and may hold dots and wildcard characters: `"OrderItems"` matches `OrderItems` but not `orderitems`, `"Sales".fct_*` matches the tables of the `Sales` schema whose names start with `fct_`, and `public."orders.v2"` names a table whose name holds a dot. `""` inside quotes is one quote. A quote inside a part, as in `odd"name`, is an ordinary character.
- `re:<regex>`: a JavaScript regular expression, always case-insensitive (a `(?-i:...)` group is refused), anchored at both ends, matched against the qualified name if it contains `\.` and against the table name otherwise.
- In a table-list file, `!<entry>` excludes; every other line includes (see Table-list files).
- `semlayer scope --tables` saves each table as its exact pattern, quoting a part that holds a quote, `*` or `?`, or a table name that holds a dot.
- Examples: `fct_*` (prefix), `*_daily` (suffix), `*orders*` (contains), `analytics.*` (a whole schema), `prod.*.orders` (three parts), `re:dim_(customer|product)s?`.

A regex is checked before it is used, and refused with the character where the problem is (counting from the `r` of `re:`) and a plain fix:

- a body (the part after `re:`) longer than 512 characters;
- a repeat of a part that holds another repeat, such as `(a+)+`, `(a*)*` or `(\w+\s?)*` (a bounded repeat may hold one when the ways to split a name stay under 1,000, as in `(_?\d{2}){0,2}`);
- a repeat of a choice whose options can start with the same character or match nothing, such as `(a|aa)+` or `(a|)+`;
- more than three unbounded repeats in a row that can trade the same characters, such as `a*a*a*a*` or `.*_.*_.*_.*` (`[a-z]+_[a-z]+_...` is fine: `_` keeps them apart; `b?` in `a*b?a*b?a*b?a*` does not, since it can match nothing), or bounded repeats and choices in a row that can split a name in more than about 262,000 ways, such as `a{0,30}a{0,30}a{0,30}a{0,30}` or twenty `a?`;
- a regex that does not compile, including a `)` that closes no group.

A glob is matched without a regex, so no glob is slow. A quote that starts a part and is never closed is refused with its character. `semlayer validate` reports every refused pattern in the files as an error at its path. A pattern that matches no table is a warning naming it: `semlayer scope` prints "Pattern "<p>" matches no table in the snapshot" and lists it in `--json` as `unmatched_patterns`.

`semlayer tables --groups` builds each schema pattern (`<schema>.*`) and prefix pattern (`<prefix>_*`) from the names, quoting a schema or prefix that a pattern cannot spell plainly (a part starting with a quote, `*` or `?`, a dot in a prefix, or a start that would read as `re:`, `!` or `#`, or white space at either end) with its case kept, and lists a group only when its pattern parses and selects exactly the group's tables. The suggested scope names single tables by their exact pattern, as `--tables` saves them.

#### Explain and match counts

Each entry of the previewed scope (the saved scope with this run's flags applied) has an origin: `scope.include[i]` or `scope.exclude[i]` (its index in the saved source file), `tables_file line <n>`, or `--include`, `--exclude` or `--tables` for an entry given now. `--json` lists them as `entries: [{ origin, entry, kind, matches, unmatched }]`: the scope lists' includes, then their excludes, then the file's lines in order; `kind` is `include` or `exclude`, `matches` the snapshot tables the entry matches on its own, and `unmatched` whether that is none. `by_schema: [{ schema, tables }]` counts the in-scope tables per schema, sorted. The human preview prints counts by schema when there are several schemas, and one line per entry with its match count, at most 20 (`--json` has every entry).

`semlayer scope --source <s> --explain <table>` takes `table`, `schema.table` or `catalog.schema.table` in any case; an unknown name is an error listing up to five close names, and a bare name in several schemas lists them. It prints whether the table is in the previewed scope, the entry that decided it, the rule, the other entries that match it, and the command that would change it: an exact `--exclude` to leave it out, or, to bring it back, every matching exclude to remove (one `--remove` per saved entry, the `--exclude` flags to drop, the table-list lines to delete) plus `--tables` when includes exist and none matches it. A name a flag would split at a comma is given as an escaped `re:` pattern, or as a table-list line when no pattern names it alone. The deciding entry is the first matching exclude (exclude wins), else the first matching include; with no include entry the table is in scope because an empty include means every table, and with includes none of which matches it is out. `--json`: `{ source, table, in_scope, decided_by: { origin, entry, kind }, also_matched: [{ origin, entry, kind }], rule }`, where `kind` is `include`, `exclude`, `empty_include` or `no_match`, and `origin` and `entry` are null for the last two.

`--reason <text>` goes with `--exclude`: it records why this command's exclude patterns leave tables out, in the owner's words (one plain line, credentials redacted), and `--write` saves it in `scope.exclude_reasons` (`{ <pattern>: <reason> }`). `--reason` without `--exclude` is refused. A pattern keeps its reason while it stays excluded; `--remove` or `--clear` drops it with the pattern. `--explain` prints `Reason recorded: <text>` for a table such a pattern excludes, and `--json` adds `reason` (or null); the preview lists each exclude with its reason.

### Table-list files

`scope.tables_file` names a table-list file, a path relative to the semantic root (`semlayer scope --from-file` writes `scope/<source>.txt`). A path that is absolute or leads outside the root, a symbolic link included, is refused.

```
# Finance tables agreed with Dana.
analytics.fct_orders
analytics.dim_*          # every dimension
re:analytics\.fct_(orders|refunds)_v\d+
!*_tmp
```

- One entry per line in the grammar above; `!<entry>` excludes. Lines end in LF or CRLF.
- `#` starts a comment at the start of a line or after a space or tab, outside a `"quoted"` part; blank lines are skipped. A `#` that follows a space inside a `re:` entry starts a comment, so write it as `\x23`.
- Each entry is checked as a scope pattern; a problem names the file, the line and the character.
- The file's entries join the source's lists: its includes add to `scope.include`, its excludes to `scope.exclude`, exclude wins, and an empty include after joining means every table. Every command that decides scope (`scope`, `tables`, `status`, `draft`, `profile`, `baseline`, `introspect`, `import`) reads the joined scope; a missing file or one with a bad entry stops it with the fix.
- `semlayer validate` errors when the file is missing (naming `semlayer scope --from-file`), is not a file, or is outside the root, and on each bad entry at `line <n>, character <c>` of the file.
- `semlayer scope --to-file` writes one exact name per selected table, sorted, under a header comment; a name that could be misread (a leading `!`, `#` or `re:`, a quote, space at an end or before `#`, a letter outside ASCII) or that differs from another table's only in case is written with both parts quoted, so the file reads back as the same selection. A name with a line break cannot be written, and `--to-file` refuses it.

## snapshots/<source>.json

Written by `semlayer introspect`, `semlayer import` and `semlayer profile`; never hand-edited except by the agent in the manual intake path.

```json
{
  "snapshot_version": 1,
  "source": "warehouse",
  "dialect": "postgres",
  "captured_at": "2026-10-26T12:00:00.000Z",
  "method": "live",
  "tables": [
    {
      "schema": "public",
      "name": "fct_orders",
      "kind": "table",
      "row_count": 10000,
      "description": null,
      "primary_key": ["order_id"],
      "unique_keys": [],
      "foreign_keys": [
        { "columns": ["customer_id"], "references": { "schema": "public", "table": "dim_customers", "columns": ["customer_id"] } }
      ],
      "profiled_at": "2026-10-26T12:05:00.000Z",
      "columns": [
        {
          "name": "status",
          "type": "varchar",
          "nullable": false,
          "description": null,
          "profile": {
            "null_fraction": 0,
            "distinct_count": 4,
            "min": null,
            "max": null,
            "top_values": [{ "value": "completed", "count": 8800 }],
            "sampled": false
          }
        },
        {
          "name": "customer_email",
          "type": "varchar",
          "nullable": true,
          "description": null,
          "profile": {
            "null_fraction": 0.01,
            "distinct_count": 1950,
            "sampled": false,
            "redacted": true
          }
        }
      ]
    }
  ]
}
```

`method` is one of `live`, `information_schema`, `dbt_manifest`, `ddl`, `manual`. `scope_state` is optional; see Scope changes. `kind` is one of `table`, `view`, `external`. `schema` is `null` when the source has no schemas. Tables are sorted by qualified name and columns keep their ordinal order. Profiles cover only the tables that were profiled. A table the last `semlayer profile` run skipped carries `profile_skipped: { "at": <ISO timestamp>, "reason": <why> }` (see Skipped tables).

### Skipped tables

`semlayer profile` runs each table's queries on their own, and `semlayer check-join` each join's. A query the database refuses skips that table or join, and everything else is written:

- The reason is one plain line: `no SELECT permission` (the role lacks `SELECT` on the table or `USAGE` on its schema); `timed out after 30 s (raise SEMLAYER_STATEMENT_TIMEOUT_MS or lower profile.sample_rows)`, with the timeout the run used (a join's names `SEMLAYER_STATEMENT_TIMEOUT_MS` only, and a source with no `profile.sample_rows` is told to set it); `its sample read no rows`; or the first line of the database's message, without control or format characters and cut at 200 characters. A Postgres error is classed by its SQLSTATE (`42501`; `57014` with "statement timeout") and any other message by a phrase outside its quoted text, so a table named `timeouts` is not a timeout. A timeout read from a results file names no `SEMLAYER_STATEMENT_TIMEOUT_MS`, which did not apply: `timed out (raise the timeout of the tool that ran the query or lower profile.sample_rows)`.
- The message is evidence, never an instruction, and it can hold a value the database read (`invalid input syntax for type integer: "<value>"`), while the reason is printed and stored. So its quoted text is kept only when every quoted piece of the line is the name of the table, its schema or one of its columns. Otherwise everything from the line's first quote to its last is shown as `"…"`, or the line is cut at its first quote when a later line holds a quote: quotes are never paired, since a value can hold one. A Postgres data exception (SQLSTATE class 22) and a DuckDB `Conversion Error` keep no quoted text at all, and what follows the word "value" outside quotes is cut (`with value …`). A value the message neither quotes nor introduces that way is not recognised, so read a skipped table's reason before committing the snapshot.
- The command lists what it skipped, each with its reason, and names the next command. `--json` carries them in `skipped`: `[{ table, reason }]` for `semlayer profile` (beside the `already profiled` entries) and `[{ model, join, reason }]` for `semlayer check-join` (beside the joins `--all` could not resolve).
- The exit code is 1 only when nothing succeeded: no table was profiled, or no join's query was answered. A connection that cannot be opened is an error as before.
- `semlayer profile` writes the snapshot even then: a skipped table keeps its earlier profile, if any, and gets `profile_skipped`, which the run that profiles it removes. `profileGaps(root, source)` in `scripts/lib/profile.ts` returns the skipped in-scope tables as `[{ table, reason }]`. A new snapshot from `semlayer introspect` or `semlayer import` carries no `profile_skipped`.
- The next `semlayer profile --source <s>` takes only the tables with no profile and the skipped ones. A skipped join keeps its earlier `evidence`, if any; check it again by name.
- A results file (`--results`) says a query failed with `{ "error": "<message>" }` in place of its rows and gives the same outcome, the reason read from the message. A result that is malformed is refused, and nothing is written, as before. A stored plan (`--plan`) holds rows only, so the `--emit-sql` note of these two commands says to finish with `--results` when the database refuses a query.

### Profiles of protected columns

`semlayer profile` stores no raw values of a protected column; `semlayer validate` warns about any that remain (an older snapshot, a column tagged later, a profile `semlayer introspect` or `semlayer import` carried over). It stores no hashed ones either: a protected column keeps counts only. A column is protected when a `pii`- or `sensitive`-tagged field reads it, directly or through a join reference (`customer.email`), when the model over its table is tagged `pii` or `sensitive`, when a model over its table lists it in `ignored_columns`, or when `sensitivityOf` in `scripts/lib/sensitivity.ts` flags it. Those rules are the ones `semlayer draft` tags by: names that look like personal data (email, phone, address, birth date, national ids, `name`, `full_name`, `first_name`, `last_name`, `middle_name`, a `*_name` whose stem is a person noun such as `customer_name` or `employee_name` (customer, user, contact, employee, owner, member, patient, person, recipient, sender), `username`, `login`, `ip`, `ip_address`, `user_agent`, `device_id`), names that look like pay, banking, card, health, credential or free-text data, and a text column that is not a key and has a different value on nearly every row, unless its name is code-like (`sku`, `code`, `*_code`, `*_number`, `*_ref`, `slug`). These rules are a heuristic: a personal column with an unusual name is profiled like any other, so review a snapshot before committing it. For a protected column, `semlayer profile` writes:

- counts only: `null_fraction` and `distinct_count`, and `min` and `max` only for a number or a date column;
- no `top_values` at all, raw or hashed: its profile query does not select them. Columns protected by name, tag or `ignored_columns` are known before profiling, so their values never leave the database; a column flagged only by its profile (a near-unique text column) has its top values dropped before the snapshot is written;
- `redacted: true` on the column's profile (`customer_email` above).

`semlayer draft` does the same for the tables it drafts as it writes their models, so the columns a draft leaves out into `ignored_columns` (a code-like `sku` that differs on every row, say) or tags keep no sample values and the draft needs no further command. `semlayer profile --source <s> --redact` rewrites the existing snapshot's profiles of protected columns this way without querying the database, for a column tagged or ignored after it was profiled, and removes the hashed top values earlier versions stored. `semlayer validate` warns while a snapshot holds raw top values or a `min` or `max` of a protected column that is not a number or a date: "<table>.<column> is protected but the snapshot holds raw sample values; run semlayer profile --source <s> --redact", and while it holds hashed top values, which a list of likely values reverses: "<table>.<column> is protected but the snapshot holds hashed top values, which a list of likely values reverses; run semlayer profile --source <s> --redact to remove them".

### Protected values in results

A query's result shows the values of a protected column as `[protected]`; nulls stay null. `--show-protected` on `semlayer run` and `semlayer goldens` shows them, for the owner alone: no role and no MCP tool passes it (see Agent roles, MCP server).

- **SQL the compiler wrote** (a golden's request, `semlayer sql`) names each result column after the field it selects, so a result column is masked when its name is protected: a field tagged `pii` or `sensitive`, a column in `ignored_columns`, a protected column of the snapshot, or a name the rules of `scripts/lib/sensitivity.ts` flag unless a `public`-tagged field has it.
- **SQL the compiler did not write** (`semlayer run`, a golden's `cross_check_sql`) can rename or wrap a column, so its result is masked by what the statement reads (`sqlMask` in `scripts/lib/output.ts`). It reads the statement's tokens, not its grammar, and fails closed:
  - A result column whose own name is protected is masked, as above, unless it is a count.
  - A count is always shown: `count(*)`, `count(email)`, `count(distinct email)`, with its `filter (...)` or `over (...)`, alone or in arithmetic with numbers and other counts.
  - A value reaches a result only through a select list (the statement's, a subquery's or a `WITH` entry's), a `VALUES` list or the arguments of a function in `FROM`. A statement that names no protected column there outside `count(...)`, and carries no value unnamed (below), is shown as above, by its column names: `where`, `on`, `group by`, `having`, `order by` and the `filter (where ...)` of an aggregate only pick, group and order rows, so `select city, sum(amount) ... where email like '%@acme.test'` shows its figures.
  - Once a statement names a protected column where a value can reach a result, a result column is shown only when it is a count or the statement shows that it reads nothing protected: every name in its select item is a column of the snapshot that is not protected, an alias the statement gives to such an item, a function or a keyword. Any other name masks the column: a protected column, an alias of an item that is itself masked, a name the snapshot does not hold. So `select email as e`, `lower(email)`, `email || ''`, `max(email)`, a `case` over it, a subquery or `WITH` entry that renames it and a field of a value built from it (`x.e` after `{'e': email} as x`) are masked, while `city` beside them is shown.
  - `select *` and `t.*` are masked by the real column names; in a statement that names a protected column, a column of `*` is shown only when it is a column of the snapshot that is not protected or an alias of an item that reads none.
  - A statement can carry a value under a name it does not write, and each of these is treated as naming a protected column: a table, its alias or any other name written among the relations of a `FROM`, used as a value (`select c from customers c`, `row_to_json(c)`, `c.to_json()`, `main.customers`); a qualified name the snapshot holds no column for, in a statement that reads a table of the snapshot, since Postgres reads `c.to_jsonb` as `to_jsonb(c)`; a `*` inside a call or with `replace`, `rename` or a pattern; a column by position (`#2`, `$1`); a column list that renames a table's columns (`customers c(a, b)`); `*` under a set operation or a column list; `SUMMARIZE`, `PIVOT`, `UNPIVOT`, `columns()`, `operator(...)`, a name written with `U&` escapes, `pg_stats` and `pg_statistic`; a function that runs a query from a string; a file read by its path in `FROM`; and any function in `FROM` other than `generate_series`, `range`, `unnest`, `duckdb_tables`, `duckdb_columns`, `duckdb_views`, `duckdb_schemas` and `pragma_table_info`, since it can read a table or a file under names of its own (`read_csv`, `pragma_storage_info`).
  - When such a statement's result columns cannot be matched to select items by name (a set operation, a column list, a function or joins in parentheses in `FROM`, `VALUES`, or a subquery's expression that is masked and has no `as` alias), only its counts are shown.
  - Result columns are matched to select items by position, for a query the CLI ran, and every alias or column name the text gives must be the result column's; rows from `--results` or `--plan` come in no known order, so they match by alias or column name alone, in a select list without `*` (a list of one item needs no name). A column that matches nothing is masked.
  - `EXPLAIN`, `DESCRIBE` and `SHOW` print no row's values and are masked by name only.
- **The note.** Masked output says why, once. When every masked column is protected by its own name: "Protected columns shown as [protected]: email, phone.", followed by how a column the owner says is not personal or sensitive data comes out of protection (its field tagged `public` in place of `pii` or `sensitive` in the turn's `semlayer apply`, an approved field revoked first, then `semlayer profile --source <s> --tables <table> --refresh`). When the statement is why: "2 columns masked: the statement reads protected column customers.email. Counts are shown; values are not. To see a column that is not protected, select it by its own name and leave the protected one out." The column is named `<table>.<column>` when the statement reads one table of the snapshot that holds it. A statement that names no protected column but carries values unnamed ends "To see a column, select it by its own name from its table, as <table>.<column>." With `--json`, `protected_columns` lists the masked result columns, `semlayer run` adds `protected_reads` (the protected columns the statement names outside `count(...)`, in a filter too), and `protected_note` holds the second form of the note when it applies.
- **Errors.** A database's error can quote a value it read (a failed cast names the text). When a statement of `semlayer run`, or a golden's `cross_check_sql`, names a protected column anywhere, inside `count(...)` or a filter too, or carries values unnamed, a failure prints no database message: "Query failed. The database's message can quote a value, and the statement reads protected column customers.email, so it is not shown." `semlayer run` still prints the message of an error raised before any row is read, which says only how the statement is written wrong: on DuckDB, a parser, binder or catalog error; on Postgres, an error of SQLSTATE class 42 that `EXPLAIN` of the statement, which reads no row, raises too (a function that looks a row's value up as a name raises class 42 as well, but only when the statement runs).
- **Where failing closed masks more than it must.** The scan knows names, not which table a name belongs to: a column that shares its name with a protected column of another table is masked, and so is every select item that reads it. A select item that reads a protected column only to test it (`case when email is null then 'no' else 'yes' end`, `length(email)`) is masked. A name that is also written among the relations of a `FROM` reads as a whole row: a `WITH` entry or subquery alias named as its own column (`with revenue as (...) select revenue from revenue`; write `revenue.revenue`), and the alias of a function in `FROM` used as its value (`generate_series(1, 3) d`; write `generate_series(1, 3) as g(d)` and select `d`). A qualified name the snapshot does not hold (`o.ctid`, a field of a struct, a column added since `semlayer introspect`, a column of a table outside the snapshot joined to one inside it) is masked. In a statement that names a protected column, a name the tool cannot place masks its column, and a statement with a set operation, a column list or a function in `FROM` shows nothing but counts. `SUMMARIZE` and a statement over any other function in `FROM` show nothing but counts, whatever they read. An error of a statement that names a protected column only in a filter or a count loses the database's message.
- **What the scan does not see.** It reads SQL as Postgres and DuckDB write strings, names and comments, also for a `cross_check_sql` in another dialect. It cannot see through a view or a function defined in the database, a table the snapshot does not hold is read by its column names alone, and the name rules are a heuristic: a personal column with an unusual name that no field tags is not protected anywhere. A confirmed golden's `expected.rows` hold what its query returns, in the clear (see goldens).

### Sampling

With `profile.sample_rows` set, a table with more rows than that is profiled over a sample. On Postgres and BigQuery the sample is `TABLESAMPLE SYSTEM (<p> PERCENT)`, with the percent computed from the table's row estimate, so it reads a share of the table's storage blocks, not the whole table (on Postgres a sample of fewer than 1,000 rows picks rows with `TABLESAMPLE BERNOULLI` instead, since a few blocks give an uneven sample); a view, an external table or a table with no row estimate cannot be read that way and its sample sorts every row, which `semlayer profile` says. A table's statistics are one query, and the top values of all its columns are one more query over the same read (or sample), grouped by each column, never one full scan per column. A BigQuery profile prints a cost warning first and suggests `--emit-sql` with a dry run of each query (`bq query --dry_run`) before running it.

When `semlayer introspect` or `semlayer import` replaces a snapshot, it compares the two and prints the changes: tables added and removed, and per table the columns added (with their types), removed and retyped. `--json` carries them as `changes`: `{ tables_added, tables_removed, columns: [{ table, added: [{ name, type }], removed: [name], retyped: [{ name, from, to }] }] }`, or `null` when there was no previous snapshot. Tables and columns match by name, exactly or else ignoring case when that is unambiguous; a type change ignores case and spacing.

### Scope changes

`semlayer introspect` and `semlayer import` record the selection in the snapshot as `scope_state: { in_scope, new_in_scope, left_scope }`, qualified table names, sorted: `in_scope` the tables in the selection (the effective scope, `scope.tables_file` included, and, when `intake.schemas` lists schemas, only theirs), `new_in_scope` the tables that entered it and have no model yet, `left_scope` the tables that left it and have an approved model. On a rerun the selection is compared with the previous snapshot's `in_scope` (with no `scope_state`, with the previous tables the current selection keeps), so a table counts as new in scope whether it is new in the database or a scope change brought it in, and as leaving whether the database dropped it or it no longer matches. The rerun prints the tables new in scope with no model and the command that drafts them (`semlayer draft --source <s> --tables <them>`; the command names the tables only when there are at most 8 and each name is letters, digits, `_` and `.`, and is otherwise `semlayer draft --source <s>`), the tables that left but are still in the database (those it dropped are named among the tables removed), and a warning for each approved model on a table that left: "left scope with approved models: <model> (<table>)", saying the models are kept and still served and how to settle each (bring the table back into scope, or revoke or remove the model). No model is changed or deleted, and while the snapshot records such a model the rerun's next command is `semlayer status`. `changes` adds `new_in_scope` and `left_scope` (table names) and `left_scope_with_models` (`[{ model, table }]`); `changes.left_scope` lists every table that left, while the snapshot's `scope_state.left_scope` keeps only those with an approved model. An entry stays in `scope_state` until it is settled (the table gets a model, or the approved model is revoked, removed or its table selected again), so a rerun before then keeps it for `semlayer status`, which lists them per source as `new_in_scope` and `left_scope_with_models`.

## Usage evidence

`semlayer usage add --source <s> <path>...` reads what people already query, as evidence for the owner's questions: which tables matter, how they join, what gets summed and which filters recur. It connects to nothing and runs none of the SQL it reads, and it stores counts and shapes in `snapshots/<s>.usage.json` (schema `../schemas/usage.schema.json`), never statement text.
