Editing a confirmed golden's question, request, `cross_check_sql`, role, user, tolerance or rows brings the same warning.

Goldens and access:

- A golden runs as its `role` and `user`; with no `role`, as the user its `user` describes, who holds every role whose `assign_when` its attributes meet, `default_role` when no other role applies, or else is the default audience. `semlayer validate` checks every golden's request with `checkRequest` in `scripts/lib/access.ts` as that user, taking every model, field and metric as approved and `access.yaml` as approved (draft items have their own warnings): each place the request names a field or metric that user may not see is an error in an approved golden and a warning in a draft one. A golden whose `role` is unknown, or an `access.yaml` with errors, is not checked this way until it is fixed.
- While `access.yaml` is a draft, every approved golden is refused, as `semlayer sql` refuses every request, and `semlayer goldens check` names reading access back with `semlayer readback --targets access` and approving it on the owner's yes as the step that resolves it.
- A golden that runs as a role is confirmed after `access.yaml` is approved, or rechecked with `semlayer goldens check --name <g>` and read back after every change to that role. `semlayer goldens confirm` warns when the golden's role is defined only in a draft `access.yaml`. A golden confirmed as a role while `access.yaml` was a draft stays valid once access is approved when its `role` and `user` are unchanged and `semlayer goldens check` passes.
- A golden may have no measures: it starts from the model of its first dimension. That is how a row-filtered model with no measures of its own, such as order lines limited through their order, is covered as the role that filters it.
- `semlayer validate` warns when a golden's `role` or `user` differ from its `confirmed_as`: what it pins changed, so run `semlayer goldens check --name <g>`, which compiles the request with that role's row filters, read the change back and confirm again on the owner's yes. No `user` and an empty `user` are the same; a golden without `confirmed_as` gets no such warning.

Personal values:

- Never quote a value of a column a `pii` or `sensitive` field reads (a name, an email) in a description, a synonym or a golden's `question` or `reference`; use the id or a count. `semlayer validate` warns when a description or synonym of a model, field, join or metric, a description in `access.yaml`, or a golden's `question` or `reference` contains a raw top value the snapshot profile still holds, of at least 3 characters and matched as a whole word in any case, of a column that a `pii`- or `sensitive`-tagged field reads. A field tagged only through its model counts only the columns it reads that look personal or sensitive by the rules `semlayer draft` tags by, so the values of a model's `status` or `region` column are not flagged.
- A protected column's profile keeps no top values (see Profiles of protected columns), so once a snapshot is redacted this check has nothing of it to match; hashed values an older snapshot holds are not matched, and `semlayer validate` asks for them to be removed. Reading a draft's text back before committing it stays the check that a name was not quoted.
- A golden's `expected.rows` are not checked: they are the answer as the golden's role, which may see the column.
- Replacing a personal value with an id in an approved golden's `question` or `reference` changes no figure and needs no new confirm: edit it and run `semlayer goldens check`. An edited `question` makes `semlayer validate` warn that no owner yes matches the golden until it is read back and confirmed again.

## .semlayer/pending/<readback_id>.json

`semlayer readback --targets a,b,c` renders a canonical plain-English readback of each approve target from the YAML and stores it here, under the semantic root, so the owner's answer can be matched to exactly what they heard. The file is committed. It never holds a protected column's values (see below).

```json
{
  "readback_id": "rb_0001",
  "created_at": "2026-01-15T12:00:00.000Z",
  "items": [
    { "n": 1, "target": "warehouse.orders.revenue", "kind": "approve",
      "text": "warehouse.orders.revenue (measure)\nQuoted description, from the YAML ...\n- The sum of amount_cents / 100.0.\n...",
      "content_hash": "sha256:f1f11e0edc5941e0b100e49cb3a3f8fd9947f70045b51eaf084b1e89edd240d9",
      "evidence": { "figure": 535381.93, "row_count": 4049 } }
  ]
}
```

- `readback_id` is `rb_` and at least four digits, the next free number after the highest one in the directory (a readback's file or a discarded one's `.discarded` marker) and in the ledger's replies (`rb_0001`, `rb_0002`, ...), so a discarded readback's id is never handed out again, and equals the file name. A readback is never overwritten; each run writes a new one.
- `created_at` comes from the command's clock (`SEMLAYER_NOW` in tests).
- `items` are numbered from 1 in the order of `--targets`, a repeated target once. `kind` is `approve`. `content_hash` is the target's content hash as it stands, the value `semlayer approve` records as `approved_hash` (see Approval hashes), so a later edit to the item shows as a changed hash. A `golden:<name>` item's `content_hash` is the golden's hash with the rows read back, and the item also holds the `tolerance` read back (see Confirming a golden requires a recorded yes) and, when its rows came from `--results <file>` or `--plan <plan_id>`, that flag as `rows_from`, which its `semlayer goldens confirm --name <g>` takes too.
- `text` is the rendered readback, in the owner's words, in this order. The item's name, then its description (a model's, field's, join's or metric's, or "No description yet"; descriptions stay outside the approval hash). The definition: a measure's aggregation and `expr`, each top-level clause of its `filter` in plain words (a filter with a top-level `or` is one clause, as `and` binds tighter than `or`; `coalesce(x, d) = d` reads "x is d or empty", a `like` pattern "starts with", "ends with" or "contains", a clause no wording fits "the condition holds: <clause>"), its `format` and `currency`; a dimension's column, type, `time_grains` and `time_zone`; a join's models, keys, how rows match and what happens to a row without a match (a bridge join's also its bridge and dedupe rule; see Bridges and multi-fact requests); a model's table, grain and primary key; a metric's formula in words; then the joins a field reads through, in the same words, its synonyms and tags. Who sees it once approved, under the current `access.yaml` (approved or draft): the default audience, and each role that sees it differently, row filters included (`semlayer access` holds the detail); it takes every model, field and metric as approved again, so an item that changed since its approval reads as it will once the owner approves it again, while `semlayer access` and the compiler keep treating it as a draft until then; it names `access.yaml` as draft when it is one, a changed `access.yaml` included, as `semlayer access` does. The block states and does not ask, and ends "Stated, not asked: who sees what is decided once, in the access step, and a change to it does not hold this approval." (a change the owner asks for is recorded with `semlayer reply --about access`). A who-sees block the same as an earlier item's in the readback is printed once and the later item reads "Who sees it once approved: the same as item <n>." The measured facts, under "Measured from the data, no need to confirm": a measure's or metric's figure (below), row counts, null rates and distinct counts of the columns the item reads (join references included), top values, and a join's evidence from `semlayer check-join`. Last, the SQL, once, under "Technical definition (the SQL, for the record)": a measure's aggregation, `expr` and `filter`, a dimension's `expr` when it is more than a column, a derived metric's `expr`, and the keys of each join the item reads through.
- The figure: for a measure or a ratio or derived metric on a connected source (a Postgres or DuckDB source in connection mode), `semlayer readback` compiles the item alone with the reference compiler, every item taken as approved and before any role's row filter, and runs it read-only with a timeout of 10 seconds (`SEMLAYER_STATEMENT_TIMEOUT_MS` when shorter): "On today's data it comes to <total> <currency>, over the <n> rows it counts" (the rows its filter keeps), and for a metric each measure it combines with its total and rows. A dimension runs no query; its top values come from the profile as above. The figure is never computed for a measure tagged `pii` or `sensitive` (or on a tagged model), nor for an aggregation other than `count` or `count_distinct` whose `expr` reads a protected column or a table the snapshot does not hold, nor for a metric that combines such a measure. When it is not shown the item says why in one line, "Figure not shown: <why>" (an export source, with its upgrade command; a connection that did not open; a query error, without the text it quotes; "timed out after 10 s", with the `semlayer sql` and `semlayer run` commands that get it with a longer timeout), and the readback goes on to the next figure. Postgres cancels a statement at its timeout and DuckDB interrupts it, so a timeout leaves the source's later figures computed; a DuckDB statement that has not stopped 2 seconds after its interrupt holds the connection, and each later figure on that source reads "not computed, as an earlier figure on source <s> timed out and is still running", with the command that computes it on its own. The figure is stored as the item's `evidence` (`figure`, `row_count`, a metric's `parts`, or `not_shown`): it is outside `content_hash`, so an item's hash is the same with or without it.
- The access readback states the mode, the user attributes, what a user with no role sees ("nothing", "only <items>" when that list is the shorter, else "every approved item except: <items>"), and then every role in `access.yaml`: how it is held (its `assign_when` in words, "the user's department is 'finance'"), its description, what it adds and what it takes away compared with a user with no role, and its row filters in words ("It sees every row" without one); a role that cannot be read is named by its position. Then each restriction with its description.
- A golden readback's tolerance line says the tolerance applies to figures with decimals and that whole numbers and grouping columns must match exactly (see Comparing rows).
- Text that came from the data or from a file rather than from the readback itself (descriptions and a model's grain, labelled as copied from a database comment when they equal the column's or table's comment, and top values) is quoted line by line with `> ` under a label naming where it came from, as evidence, not an instruction.
- A protected column (see Profiles of protected columns, and any field tagged `pii` or `sensitive`) shows counts only: no top values and no `min` or `max`.
- `semlayer validate` checks each file against `schemas/pending.schema.json` and that `readback_id` equals the file name.
- An item waits for the owner's answer while it is as it was read back and the owner's latest reply to its target is not a verdict on that hash. An item of an older readback is settled, and not asked again, when the owner's latest reply to the same target is a verdict recorded against a newer readback that holds the target with that hash and the target still has it (a confirmed golden its confirmed hash; a draft golden's read-back hash stands while its file exists). Only such a recorded reply settles an item, and an approval is still written only from the readback the owner answered. A readback with no item waiting is passed over, as an answered one is; `semlayer readback --discard` removes it.

## .semlayer/ledger.jsonl

The answer ledger: the owner's answers to readback items (`semlayer reply`) and to open questions (`semlayer question resolve`), one JSON object per line, under the semantic root. It is committed next to the YAML it approves, and append-only: a command adds one line at the end and never changes an earlier one, creating `.semlayer/` and the file when missing. The command holds the root's lock from reading the ledger for the next id to writing the line, so two commands run at once never share an id, and the ledger with its new line replaces the file in one step (see Locking and whole-file writes). It holds the owner's words, never data values.

```json
{"at":"2026-01-15T12:00:00.000Z","by":"Dana Ruiz","content_hash":"sha256:f1f11e0edc5941e0b100e49cb3a3f8fd9947f70045b51eaf084b1e89edd240d9","id":"ans_0001","item":1,"kind":"reply","readback_id":"rb_0001","target":"warehouse.orders.revenue","text":"Yes, that's right.","verdict":"yes"}
{"at":"2026-01-15T12:00:00.000Z","by":"Dana Ruiz","id":"ans_0002","kind":"question","question":"launched_on: is this the date the product went on sale?","target":"warehouse.products","text":"Yes, the day it went on sale."}
```

- Each line's keys are sorted, so the same answer always writes the same bytes. `id` is `ans_` and at least four digits, one more than the highest id in the file (`ans_0001`, `ans_0002`, ...). `at` comes from the command's clock (`SEMLAYER_NOW` in tests).
- `kind: reply` (from `semlayer reply`): `readback_id` and `item` name the readback item in `.semlayer/pending/`, `target` is its approve target (`golden:<name>` for a golden) and `content_hash` the hash the item stored when it was read back, so an answer is tied to exactly what the owner heard. `verdict` is `yes`, `no`, `change` or `null` (words recorded without a verdict, which no approval can use). `about: access`, only with `verdict: change` (from `semlayer reply --about access`), marks a change to who sees the item, not to the item: every reader of the owner's latest reply to a target (`semlayer approve`, `semlayer goldens confirm`, `semlayer status`, `semlayer next`, `semlayer validate`) skips such a line, so it neither approves the item nor overrides a yes or a no to it, and it stays open for the access phase until a later line is a yes to `access` (see semlayer next --json).
- `kind: question` (from `semlayer question resolve`, or `semlayer review apply`): `target` is the ref the question was on (`source.model`, `source.model.field` or `access`, and from a review form also `access.roles.<role>`) and `question` the open question as the file held it; it has no `readback_id`, `item`, `content_hash` or `verdict`.
- `text` is the owner's words as given, with line breaks and tabs turned to spaces, control characters and ANSI escapes removed, and anything matching the credential patterns of `scripts/lib/credentials.ts` (the user and password of a URL, a credential-named key such as `password`, `token`, `secret` or `AccountKey` set to a value, a bearer token) replaced by `[redacted]`. The command then says so and asks for the credential to be changed. `by` is folded to one line and redacted the same way.
- A command refuses to append, writing nothing, when a line of the file is not a JSON object or the last line has no line break, when the file is a symbolic link, or when `.semlayer` is a link that points nowhere or outside the semantic root.
- `semlayer validate` checks each line against `schemas/ledger.schema.json` and reports a line that is not JSON, a last line without a line break, an id used twice, an id lower than one before it, and a ledger that is not a plain file inside the root (it or its `.semlayer` directory is a link). That earlier lines were never changed cannot be checked: version control shows it.

## Iterating a layer

### The drift record

`semlayer introspect` keeps `.semlayer/drift.json` under the semantic root (`schemas/drift.schema.json`), so a schema change stays on record after the snapshot is replaced. It is committed. It holds table, column and type names, the approve targets of fields, content hashes and ledger ids: never a data value, a URL or a credential.

```json
{
  "version": 1,
  "sources": [
    {
      "source": "warehouse",
      "at": "2026-01-15T12:00:00.000Z",
      "diff": { "tables_added": [], "tables_removed": [], "columns": [
        { "table": "acme.fct_orders", "added": [], "removed": ["shipping_cents"], "retyped": [{ "name": "amount_cents", "from": "integer", "to": "numeric(12,2)" }] } ] },
      "retyped": [
        { "table": "acme.fct_orders", "column": "amount_cents", "from": "integer", "to": "numeric(12,2)", "at": "2026-01-15T12:00:00.000Z",
          "reads": [{ "ref": "warehouse.orders.revenue", "hash": "sha256:f1f11e0edc5941e0b100e49cb3a3f8fd9947f70045b51eaf084b1e89edd240d9", "yes": "ans_0003" }] }
      ]
    }
  ]
}
```

- One entry per source, sorted by source. `at` and `diff` are those of the last introspect that found a change against the previous snapshot (`diff` has the shape of `changes` in `semlayer introspect --json`; see Scope changes); a run that finds none leaves them. The first introspect of a source has no previous snapshot and records nothing.
- A table the connection's role can no longer read leaves the snapshot and is listed in `tables_removed` like a dropped one; `unreadable` names those of them the database still holds but the role cannot read (a Postgres source on a live run), so the fix is a grant and another introspect, not an edit of the layer.
- `retyped` lists the retyped columns still open. When an introspect finds a column retyped, it records each approved dimension or measure whose `expr` or `filter` names the column (the model's table found in the snapshot as `semlayer status` finds it, the names read as the column-usage checks read them) with its content hash and the id of the owner's latest yes to it in the ledger (`null` when there is none). The column stays open for that field until the field is edited (its content hash changes) or approved again (a newer yes from the owner in the ledger); the next introspect drops the fields that closed, and a column no field keeps open. A column retyped again keeps its first `from` and `at`, and closes when it returns to that type; a column the table no longer has closes too (a field reading it is then a missing column).
- `semlayer validate` warns `release.column_retyped` on each approved field still open, naming the column, its old and new types and when the change was found, with `next` `semlayer readback --targets <ref>`. A record that does not parse or match its schema is skipped by `semlayer validate` and replaced by the next introspect without a message. `semlayer import` of an export does not write the record, so a column retyped in a fresh export gets no warning. A field is matched by the bare column name in its `expr` or `filter`; a table-qualified reference such as `orders.amount_cents` is not matched.
- The record is written whole (see Locking and whole-file writes) under the root's lock, before the snapshot, and only when it changed, so a second identical introspect leaves its bytes as they were. A failed introspect leaves the record as it was. When `.semlayer` is a link that leads outside the semantic root or nowhere, the introspect refuses and writes neither the record nor the snapshot.

### Layer version and changelog

`layer_version` in `semantic.yaml` (`major.minor`, such as `1.1`) is the version of the layer as last released; the owner chooses it, and the tool checks that it rises. `changelog.yaml` beside `semantic.yaml` (`schemas/changelog.schema.json`, types in `scripts/lib/release-types.ts`) holds one entry per release, oldest first. A layer with neither has never been released, which is every layer built before releases existed: `semlayer validate` says nothing about it.

```yaml
version: 1
entries:
  - version: "1.1"
    date: 2026-02-03
    note: Added the refund rate.
    items:                       # approve target to content hash, every approved item at the release
      warehouse.orders.revenue: sha256:f1f1...
    goldens:                     # golden name to its recorded rows
      revenue_by_month: [{ month: "2025-01", revenue: 120000 }]
    added: [metric:refund_rate]
    changed: []
    removed: []
    goldens_moved:               # goldens whose rows differ from the last entry's, with cell counts
      - { golden: revenue_by_month, before: 24, after: 24, changed: 2 }
    goldens_unconfirmed: [aov_2025] # optional: in the last entry, a draft now (semlayer goldens unconfirm)
    goldens_removed: [old_check]    # optional: in the last entry, its file gone (semlayer goldens remove)
```

- Append-only. `appendEntry` (`scripts/lib/release.ts`) replaces the file whole (temp file and rename, under the root's lock) and refuses a version that is not above the last entry's, comparing `major` and `minor` as numbers (1.10 is above 1.9), so one version is never recorded twice. `semlayer change close` is the command that calls it. The hook refuses a hand edit or shell write of `changelog.yaml` as it refuses one of the ledger (see Hand edits), because later releases compare against the hashes and rows an entry records.
- An entry holds approve targets, content hashes, golden names and the rows a golden file already holds, never a connection URL, a password or any other data value. Golden rows are recorded as the golden file stores them: a confirmed golden's `expected.rows` hold what its query returned, in the clear, a protected column's values included (see Protected values in results); the changelog copies those rows and adds no value the golden does not hold, so a protected column the golden holds is as visible in the changelog as in the golden.
- `semlayer validate` reports `release.changelog_malformed` (an error naming `changelog.yaml`) when the file is not valid YAML, does not match its schema or has versions that do not rise, and `release.version_mismatch` (an error on `semantic.yaml`) when `layer_version` is not the version of the last entry, set without a changelog or absent beside one.

### Change status

`semlayer change status` compares the layer as it stands with the last entry of `changelog.yaml` and writes nothing. Each item's current content hash is the one `semlayer approve` records (`statusDetail` in `scripts/lib/status.ts`), so whatever a hash covers (a measure's segment filters, a join's `through`, a schema pair's keys) makes the item changed when it is edited. The map holds models, fields, joins, metrics, `access`, schema pairs as `schema-pair:<source>:<from>:<to>` and, for a source with `intake.primary` set, `primary:<source>`; goldens are not in it. A `primary:<source>` item is never a draft and is not an approve target, so it never blocks a close. A changed join lists the approved goldens that read any field of the model it leaves or the model it reaches. When items are draft, `next` names the first draft; run the command again for the rest.

- `added` and `removed` are refs the entry lacks or has and the layer no longer does. `changed` lists refs in both whose hash differs, each with `state` (`approved` when the item is approved at its new content, `draft` when it is a draft or was edited since its approval) and `affected_goldens`: the approved goldens whose request reads it, by name. A golden reads a field its request names, a metric and the measures the metric combines, and the measures that list a segment the request names; a changed model is read by a golden that reads any of its fields.
- With no changelog the command exits 0 and says there is no release yet, naming `semlayer change close --version 1.0`. A changelog that does not read is refused as `semlayer validate` reports it.
- `drift` is `.semlayer/drift.json` as it is, or `null`; the plain output lists each source's last schema change and the retyped columns still open.
- The output holds refs, hashes and names, never a data value; golden rows stay in the changelog.

### The change round

A layer is released first and changed after: a layer with no changelog is led to its first release (`semlayer change close --version 1.0`), and `semlayer change start` is refused on it (`release.change_unreleased`). On a released layer, `semlayer change start --note <text> --by <name>` opens a round: it writes `.semlayer/change.json` (`schemas/change.schema.json`), whole (temp file and rename), under the root's lock. The file holds `version: 1`, the `note` (what the owner wants changed, in their words, up to 500 characters), `started_by`, `started_at` (the command's clock, `SEMLAYER_NOW` in tests) `from_version` (the last release in `changelog.yaml`) and `drafts_at_start` (the refs of the drafts never approved that are not in the last release when the round starts: the ones the owner deferred); never a data value or a connection detail. A second `change start` while the file exists is refused (`release.change_open`), naming `semlayer change status`; a missing or empty `--note` or `--by` is refused (`release.change_missing_input`), a note over 500 characters or a name over 200 is refused (`release.change_too_long`), and a `change.json` that does not read is refused (`release.change_malformed`, which `semlayer validate` reports as an error naming the file). The hook refuses a hand edit or shell write of the file as it refuses the ledger and `changelog.yaml` (see Hand edits); `semlayer change close` removes it.

- `semlayer status --json` gains `layer_version` (semantic.yaml's, or `null` before the first release) and `change` (the file's content, or `null`). A round does not change how a layer still being built is read: phase `change` replaces only `done`, so every phase before it (`reapprove`, `metrics`, `verify`, and the rest) keeps its own steps while a round is open.
- Phase `change` is returned while `change` is not `null` and every earlier phase is complete; its `next_step.action` is `semlayer change status`. `semlayer next` names there, one at a time, the readback of the items the round added or changed that are not yet approved (a metric added in the round included), except a draft already present when the round started and listed in `drafts_at_start` (the owner deferred it; the close leaves it out, see Closing a round), and any item the last release held that is not approved as it stands (up to eight in one readback; its `phase` is `metrics` for a metric, whose card holds how a metric is read back, else `change`). Then the close: a `run` action `semlayer change close --version <next minor>` (1.0 to 1.1), whose `why` holds the question to ask the owner first, since the owner chooses the version (a large change could be the next major). When confirmed goldens read an item changed since the release (its `affected_goldens`) and the owner has not confirmed them since the change, the `why` names up to eight of them, says to run `semlayer goldens check` before asking for the version, and names the two ways to settle one whose figures moved: the owner's yes to the new rows, or `semlayer goldens unconfirm --name <g>` for figures the owner cannot check now. One that passes the check needs no new yes; the close reruns them and refuses while one that moved is still confirmed on the old figures. `next` runs no query: a golden counts as confirmed since the change when it is a draft, or when the owner's latest yes to it as it stands is newer in the ledger (a higher `ans_` id) than the yes to each changed item it reads. Closing a round is the last step of every round (see Closing a round).
- On a finished layer with no round open, `done`'s message and `next`'s `why` end with how to go on: with no changelog, recording the first release (`semlayer change close --version 1.0`); with one, `semlayer change start --note "<what the owner wants changed>" --by "<owner>"`. The action stays `validate`, kind `done`.
- An edit to an approved item sends it back to draft until the owner's recorded yes (`reapprove`, see Approval hashes); the round adds no approval path of its own.

### Closing a round

`semlayer change close --version <major.minor>` records a release. With no `changelog.yaml` it records the first (1.0, or the version the owner chose), with no round open; on a released layer it closes the open round, and with none open it is refused (`release.close_no_round`, naming `semlayer change start`). It holds the root's lock from its first read to its last write. A missing `--version` is refused (`release.close_missing_version`), one not written `major.minor` (`release.version_invalid`), and one not above the last entry's (`release.version_not_above`, naming the next minor), so closing twice with one version fails.

- Drafts left out. A draft never approved does not block the close: an item that is not approved, carries no trace of an approval (no `approved_by`, `approved_at`, `approved_hash` or `last_approved`) and has no entry in the last release's `items` is left out of the release, which holds only items approved as they stand (`primary:<source>` as it is). The close prints the drafts left out (`--json`: `drafts_left_out`, refs only) with one sentence saying they are not part of this release and join a later one once the owner approves them. A left-out draft is not in the entry's `items`, `added` or `changed`; once the owner approves it, the next release lists it under `added`. A draft golden is left out of `goldens` the same way and blocks nothing. A golden the last entry recorded that is a draft now (returned to draft with `semlayer goldens unconfirm`, because its figures moved and the owner has not confirmed the new ones) is left out of `goldens` and named in the entry's `goldens_unconfirmed`, and one whose file is gone (`semlayer goldens remove` after the unconfirm) in `goldens_removed`; the close prints each list with a sentence saying the last release held them, and `--json` gives both lists, empty when there are none. Once the owner confirms it again, a later release records the golden in `goldens` again. `semlayer next` is stricter than the close: it asks for the readback of a draft made in the round (a metric the owner asked for) before offering the close, so in practice a release leaves out the drafts the owner deferred, and names them.
- Blockers. The close is refused (`release.close_blocked`, exit 1), writing nothing, while an approved item changed since its approval is not approved again, while an item the last release held, or one that carries an approval's trace, is now a draft, while a confirmed golden reads a left-out draft (`semlayer validate` only warns, `golden.uses_draft`, so the close refuses: its recorded rows would rest on an item the owner has not approved; read the draft back, or return the golden to draft), while `semlayer validate` has an error, or while an affected golden's figures moved from its recorded rows (the message names both ways to settle it: the owner's yes to the new rows, read back and recorded with `semlayer goldens confirm`, or `semlayer goldens unconfirm --name <g>` when the owner cannot check the new figures now, which leaves the golden out of the release). A moved golden that is still confirmed blocks the close. Removing an item the last release held is no blocker; it is listed under `removed`. The message lists every blocker with the command that settles it, and `next` is the first one's. An affected golden is an approved golden in `affected_goldens` of an item changed since the last release (see Change status); the first release has none.
- Rerun. The close reruns each affected golden with the rows `semlayer readback` and `semlayer goldens confirm` use (`goldenRun` in `scripts/commands/goldens.ts`, read-only through the source's adapter) and compares them with its recorded `expected.rows` as `semlayer goldens check` does (`compareRows` at the golden's tolerance), protected columns shown as `[protected]`. Rows that differ block the close until the owner's yes to the new rows is recorded with `semlayer goldens confirm`; the close itself approves and confirms nothing. A golden whose query does not run blocks it too, naming `semlayer goldens check --name <g>`, without the database's message.
- Not rerun. A golden on a source that cannot be queried now (an export source, a dialect semlayer does not connect to, a connection whose variables are not set, or one that does not open) is not rerun: the close lists it under `not_rerun` with the reason and says its figures were not compared with its recorded rows. It neither blocks the close nor counts as unchanged.
- The entry. `items` holds the items of the change status map that are approved as they stand, with the hashes `semlayer approve` records, and never a draft's hash; `goldens` holds every approved golden's `expected.rows` as its file stores them; `added`, `changed` and `removed` are the change status lists; `goldens_moved` lists each golden whose recorded rows differ from the last entry's, with cells before, after and that differ (rows pair by position for an ordered golden, else by their grouping columns; a row with no partner counts all its cells); `goldens_unconfirmed` and `goldens_removed` (see Drafts left out) are written only when they are not empty. `note` is the round's note, `First release.` for a first release, and `date` the command's clock.
- Order of writes. `layer_version` in `semantic.yaml` first (the YAML writer, comments kept), then the entry (`appendEntry`), then the round's file is removed. A close stopped after the first write leaves `layer_version` above the last entry: `semlayer validate` reports `release.version_mismatch`, naming the same `semlayer change close --version <v>`, which finishes the release (the close does not count that one mismatch as a blocker). Stopped after the second, the round's file is left with a `from_version` below the last release: the next `semlayer change close`, whatever its `--version`, removes it, says the round was already recorded, and writes nothing else (`--json`: `{ closed, version, already_recorded: true, layer_version, next }`).
- Output. Plain: the version, the date, what was added, changed and removed, the goldens that moved with their cell counts, the goldens not rerun, and the next step. `--json`: `{ closed, version, date, note, file, items, added, changed, removed, goldens_moved, goldens_unconfirmed, goldens_removed, not_rerun: [{ golden, source, reason }], drafts_left_out, layer_version, next }`, where `items` is the number of items in the release and `drafts_left_out` lists refs. Neither shows a golden's rows; names, refs and counts only.

## Review forms

`semlayer review export --readback <readback_id> --format md|json` writes a pending readback as a form the owner fills in, to `.semlayer/reviews/<readback_id>.<md|json>` by default. It holds nothing the pending readback and the YAML do not already hold, so it never holds a protected column's values. The JSON form (schema `review.schema.json`):

```json
{
  "version": 1,
  "readback_id": "rb_0001",
  "created_at": "2026-01-15T12:00:00.000Z",
  "items": [
    { "n": 1, "target": "warehouse.orders.revenue",
      "content_hash": "sha256:f1f11e0edc5941e0b100e49cb3a3f8fd9947f70045b51eaf084b1e89edd240d9",
      "text": "warehouse.orders.revenue (measure)\n- The sum of amount_cents / 100.0.\n...",
      "verdict": null, "answer": "" }
  ],
  "questions": [
    { "ref": "warehouse.customers", "key": "q_3c1f0e9a2b7d4c55", "question": "Which date is the signup date?", "answer": "" }
  ]
}
```

- `items` are the readback's items in order, each with its number, target, `content_hash` and `text` exactly as `.semlayer/pending/<readback_id>.json` holds them. `verdict` is `yes` (right as written), `no` (wrong), `change` (right once something changes) or `null` while unanswered; `answer` is the owner's own words, empty while unanswered.
- `questions` are the open questions of what the items belong to, in item order without repeats: a model's or a field's model, the models a metric's measures are on, and for `access` the top-level questions of `access.yaml` and each role's. `ref` is `source.model`, `access` or `access.roles.<role>`; `key` is `q_` and the SHA-256 of the question as stored, cut to 16 hex characters, so the question can be found again after the form returns.
- `created_at` comes from the command's clock (`SEMLAYER_NOW` in tests); the same readback, YAML and clock give the same bytes.
- The Markdown form says at the top how to fill it in and that only the verdicts and answers are read back in. Its first marker, `<!-- semlayer-review {"version":1,"readback_id":...,"created_at":...} -->`, names the readback. Each item is a `## Item <n>: <target>` heading, a marker `<!-- semlayer-item {"n":...,"target":...,"content_hash":...} -->`, the readback text with every line behind `> ` (so no line of it, data-borne text included, can pass for a marker or a fill-in line), a line `Verdict: [ ] yes  [ ] no  [ ] change` and a line `Answer:` followed by an empty fenced block. Open questions follow under `## Open questions`, each a `### Question <i>: <ref>` heading, a marker `<!-- semlayer-question {"ref":...,"key":...} -->`, the file that holds it, the question behind `> ` and the same `Answer:` block.
- Reading a Markdown form back (`parseReviewMarkdown` in `scripts/lib/review.ts`) gives the JSON form's structure: the texts from the quoted lines, a verdict from the one box marked with anything but spaces (`[x]`, `[ x ]`, `[✓]`), or the bare word yes, no or change left on the `Verdict:` line, and an answer from the lines after `Answer:` up to the next heading or marker, without the fence around them and without a fenced block left empty. More than one verdict, other text on the `Verdict:` line, a changed or repeated marker, or a result the schema refuses is an error.
- `semlayer review export` refuses a readback id that is unknown or not of the form `rb_0001`, and a readback whose item changed since it was read back (its content hash is no longer the target's), naming the `semlayer readback` that makes a fresh one. `--out` is resolved from the semantic root and must end in the format's extension; a path outside the root (also through a link), under `sources/`, `snapshots/`, `models/`, `goldens/`, `.semlayer/pending/` or `.semlayer/plans/`, a link or a directory is refused. An existing file is never overwritten without `--force`, and even with it only when the file is a review form. A form the schema would refuse (a YAML open question that is empty, say) is not written; the message names `semlayer validate`.
- `semlayer validate` checks each `.semlayer/reviews/*.json` against `schemas/review.schema.json`.

### Applying a filled form

`semlayer review apply <file> --by <owner>` reads a filled form (Markdown or JSON by its extension, from a path resolved from the working directory, which it only reads) and records its answers in `.semlayer/ledger.jsonl`. It never approves: `semlayer approve` on a recorded yes does.

- The form must parse (Markdown, as above) or match `review.schema.json` (JSON), its `readback_id` must be in `.semlayer/pending/`, and each item must be an item of that readback with the same target, once; a question appears once. Otherwise the command records nothing.
- Only each item's `verdict` and `answer` and each question's `answer` are read; the texts are not, so text from the data in the form cannot pass as a verdict or an answer.
- An item with a verdict or an answer is recorded as `semlayer reply` records it (a `kind: reply` line: the pending item's target and content hash, the answer as `text` with credentials redacted and control characters removed, the verdict, `by` and the time). A verdict without words records the verdict word as `text`; words without a verdict record `verdict: null`. The item is refused, and nothing recorded for it, when its `content_hash` in the form is not the pending item's or the target's hash no longer is (or the target is gone): the message says it changed since the form was exported and names `semlayer readback --targets <t>` and `semlayer review export` for a new form.
- A question with an answer is found by `ref` and `key` among the open questions its ref holds now; it is removed (every copy, when the file holds it twice) and recorded as `semlayer question resolve` does (the file changes as a typed edit does, and a `kind: question` line is appended). A question no longer open (resolved or edited since export) records nothing and is reported.
- Applying a form again records nothing new: an item whose `readback_id`, `item`, `verdict` and `text` a reply line already holds is reported as already recorded, and a question whose ref, answer and question text or key a question line already holds as already resolved (removed from the YAML if it is still there, with no new line).
- Everything is checked before anything is written. The ledger lines are appended first, items in form order, then questions, and the YAML edits written after them; a failure part-way removes the lines this run appended and puts every edited file back, so the ledger and the YAML are as they were. A run stopped between the two leaves answers recorded with their questions still open, which applying the form again finishes.
- An item of the readback or an open question of its targets that the form leaves out (deleted from it, or added since export) still needs an answer.
- The report lists what was recorded, already recorded, refused and why, the questions not open any more and what still needs an answer, then the next commands: `semlayer approve --target <t> --by "<owner>"` for each yes on an unchanged target (`semlayer goldens confirm --name <g>` for a golden, after the upgrade to a connection when its source is an export source, named once per source), a typed edit and `semlayer readback --targets <t>` for a no or a change, the `semlayer reply` that adds a verdict to words without one, and `semlayer review apply` again once the owner answers the rest.

## Reference compiler

`semlayer sql` compiles a request, in the goldens' request format (see goldens above; `scripts/lib/compiler/`), to SQL for a `postgres` or `duckdb` source. It never runs the SQL; `semlayer run` and `semlayer goldens` run queries. This version compiles the measures of one model, the base model, with dimensions and filters on it and on the models it reaches through to-one joins, the measures of several models (see Bridges and multi-fact requests), and ratio and derived metrics over the measures of one or more models (see metrics.yaml):

- The request is checked against `request` in `golden.schema.json`, then with `checkRequest` (`scripts/lib/access.ts`) as the user `--role` and `--user` describe, or the default audience: a draft or hidden field, an unknown field and a draft `access.yaml` are refused, each with its reason.
  - A field or metric hidden only while it, its model or a measure it reads awaits approval (a draft, an approved item changed since its approval, or one missing the hash other items carry) is refused in plain words that name what it waits on and its readback, with no reason code: "measures[0] names orders.revenue, which changed since approval; treated as draft until it is read back and approved again: semlayer readback --targets warehouse.orders.revenue, then semlayer approve --target warehouse.orders.revenue --by "<owner>" after the owner's yes", and for a metric "measures[0] names net_revenue, which is held back because it reads orders.revenue, which changed since approval; it is served once orders.revenue is read back and approved again: semlayer readback --targets warehouse.orders.revenue, then ...". The refusal's `awaits` lists those approve targets, and when only such refusals hide what the request names, `next` is their readback: "Read warehouse.orders.revenue back to the owner with semlayer readback --targets warehouse.orders.revenue and approve it on their yes, then run semlayer sql again."
  - A field or metric an access rule hides from this user, alone or with an item awaiting approval, keeps "<position>[<i>] names <ref>, which this user may not see (<reasons>)", since approval alone would not show it.
- Each requested dimension is selected as its `expr`, named as the field; a time dimension with a grain, `model.field:grain`, is `date_trunc('<grain>', <expr>)`, cast to a date for `day` and coarser grains and kept a timestamp for `hour`, and is named `<field>_<grain>` (`order_date_month`). The grain must be one of the dimension's `time_grains`. The expr is truncated as written: it already yields the wall-clock time in the zone days are counted in (`time_zone` names the zone a column without one is recorded in; see Time zones), and a value with a time zone buckets in the session time zone, UTC. The SQL assumes that session time zone, as the CLI's connections set it, and `--json` says so as `session_time_zone: "UTC"`.
- Each measure is selected as its aggregate, named as the measure: `sum`, `avg`, `min`, `max` over its `expr`, `count(<expr>)` or `count(*)` without one, and `count(distinct <expr>)` for `count_distinct`. A measure's `filter` applies inside its aggregate, `<agg>(...) filter (where <filter>)`, so it narrows only that measure.
- Request filters combine with `AND`: a filter on a dimension (with a grain, on the truncated value) goes in `WHERE`, one on a measure in `HAVING`. Every filter value is a bound parameter, `$1`, `$2`, ... in the order the SQL reads them on both dialects, listed in `params`; no value enters the SQL text. `in` and `not_in` bind one parameter per element, `between` two, and `is_null` and `not_null` none.
- The query groups by every requested dimension. `order_by` names a requested field (a time dimension without its grain when the request selects it at one grain) and sorts with nulls last; `limit` limits the rows.
- A dimension expr or measure expr or filter holding `--` is placed as `(<text>` and a newline then `)`, so a line comment in it ends there.
- The base model is the model of the request's measures when they share one model (with no measures, the model of its first dimension); measures of several models compile in parts, as a metric does. A dimension, or a filter on a dimension, of another model (`customers.region`) reaches it through `many_to_one` and `one_to_one` joins from the base model: the shortest path, at most two hops, and it must be the only one that short. Two shortest paths are refused (`ambiguous_join_path`), naming both. With no such path, one bridge join of the base model straight to the model reaches it (see Bridges and multi-fact requests); no path, or one only through a `one_to_many` join, which would repeat the base model's rows (fan-out), is refused (`no_join_path`), naming that join. Each hop of a path is joined once, with its declared `type` (`left` by default) and its `keys`, whatever number of fields use it. A filter on another model's measure is refused.
- A dimension expr or measure filter that reads a join reference, `<join>.<column>` or `<join>.<join>.<column>`, adds each hop of the chain as a join of its own, chained in order with each join's declared `type` (see Join references and Definition joins): apart from a request path along the same declared join, applied whatever the role may see and without the target's row filters. Fields that read through the same chain share its joins. A measure `expr` that reads a join reference, and a join reference through a `one_to_many` join, are refused as layer errors.
- Every join the query adds is to-one. One whose current evidence shows fan-out (`right_key_unique: false`, or `max_fanout` above 1) is refused (`join_fans_out`), naming `semlayer check-join`, unless the join has `fanout_override`; evidence that shows `many_to_many` is refused whatever `fanout_override` says, since the join needs a bridge table. Evidence measured for another target or other keys is stale (`semlayer validate` warns) and refuses nothing.
- A query that reads only the base table writes each expr as it is. A query with joins names the base table by its model name (`"orders"`), a model a request path reaches by the path (`"orders.customer"`, `"orders.customer.segment"`), and a definition join by its owner's alias, `~` and the join chain (`"orders~test_account"`, `"refunds~order.test_account"`). It qualifies each column of an expr or filter with its table's alias and each join reference with its join's alias, and keeps the rest of the text, comments included, as written. A bare name is a column unless it is SQL syntax: a keyword (interval units too, as in `interval 30 days`), a function name, a cast's type, a literal's type (`date '2025-01-01'`) or a lambda parameter; `->>`, and `->` before a string or a number, are Postgres's JSON operators and bind no lambda parameter (`coalesce(meta->>'k', 'none')` reads the column `meta`). When the snapshot lists the table's columns, a bare name is a column exactly when it is one of them, so a column named like a keyword (`year`) is qualified too. Join keys are written as the snapshot spells them. The joins follow `from` in the order the query first reads them: the selected columns, then `WHERE`, then `HAVING`.
- The user's row filters apply as `evaluateLayer` (`scripts/lib/access.ts`) evaluates them for the user (see Row filters): on the base model in `WHERE`, before the request's filters; on a model a request path reaches, in that join's `ON` clause, so a `left` join keeps the base row with the joined columns blank and an `inner` join drops it. A definition join and a hierarchy filter's closure model never carry one. One role's filters on a model combine with `AND` and the held roles' with `OR`; a role that lacks an attribute its filter needs contributes no rows, and a model of which the user sees no rows reads `false` (`where false`, or `on <keys> and false`). The plain output names each row filter applied and says when the user sees no rows of a model.
- Each `{{ user.<attribute> }}` is a bound parameter: one for a single value, and one per element for a list, written `($1, $2)` after `IN`, or bare where the expr already writes parentheses around the placeholder. A row filter whose text does not balance its parentheses or holds a parameter marker or semicolon (see Row filters), a placeholder inside a string or a comment, and a list read anywhere but after `IN` are refused as layer errors; a value that is an object, or a list holding one, and a hierarchy id that is not a number or a non-empty text, as an invalid request. Parameters are numbered in reading order: the `ON` clauses in join order, then `WHERE`, then `HAVING`.
- A hierarchy filter is `(<column> = $1 or <column> in (select "<via>"."<member>" from <via table> as "<via>" where "<via>"."<manager>" = $2))`, the user's id bound once per use (without `include_self`, only the `in` part), the closure model aliased by its model name inside its subquery. The closure model must be a model of the filtered model's source.
- A row filter's join references add definition joins. On the base model they are joins of the query, shared with fields that read the same chain. On a model a request path reaches they nest inside that path's join, `left join (<customers> as "orders.customer" left join <regions> as "orders.customer~row_filter~home" on ...) on <keys> and (<filter>)`, aliased by the path, `~row_filter~` and the chain, so the `ON` clause can read them and a `left` join still keeps the rows the filter leaves out.
- A request that names a metric (in `measures`, `filters` or `order_by`), or whose measures come from several models, compiles in parts. Each model whose measures the request's metrics and measures use is one part, in the order the request first needs them: a CTE named `"<model> measures"`, compiled as a request for those measures alone by the rules above, grouped by the request's dimensions and with its filters on dimensions. With one part, the dimensions are those the request names, through its to-one paths. With several, a part groups by the dimension as the request names it when its model is the dimension's model or reaches it through one path of to-one joins, as a request path (`orders.region` in the refunds part is `"refunds.order".region`), so every part groups by the same column; a part's model that reaches it by no such path groups by its own dimension of the same name (`orders.region` is `visits.region` in the visits part). A part's model that has neither is refused (`no_shared_dimension`), naming the dimension and the model, as is a filter on such a dimension. The parts combine on the dimensions: a CTE `"request groups"` is the `union` of every part's dimension columns, and each part is left joined to it with `is not distinct from` on each column, so a group any part has appears once, null groups included. Without dimensions each part is one row and they are cross joined. The base model, `model` in the output, is the first part's.
- A ratio is `<numerator> / nullif(<denominator>, 0)` and a derived metric its `expr` with each `{model.measure}` replaced by that part's column, every division as `nullif(<divisor>, 0)`; a zero or missing divisor gives null. On postgres each division casts its dividend to `numeric`, so two counts do not divide as integers. Inside a metric, a `sum`, `count` or `count_distinct` measure other than a divisor is `coalesce(<column>, 0)`, so a group a part has no rows for, or whose measure filter leaves none, counts as 0. A selected measure, and a measure in the outer `WHERE`, is its part's column as it is (null for a group its part has no rows for, so such a filter leaves that group out), named as the measure; a selected metric is named as the metric, with `kind: "metric"` in `columns`. A filter on a metric, or on a measure of a model the request uses, goes in the outer query's `WHERE`; parameters are numbered in reading order, every part's in turn, then the outer `WHERE`. `order_by` (by column position, nulls last) and `limit` apply to the outer query. A metric of a type other than `ratio` or `derived` is refused (`unsupported_metric`), naming its type. Each part applies the user's row filters as a one-model request does, the base in its `WHERE` and a model its path reaches in the join's `ON` clause, so every measure a metric reads counts only the rows the user may see; its parameters, row filters' first, are numbered in the part's turn.
- The same request, layer and user always give the same SQL text, parameters and joins. The compiled SQL passes the read-only check `semlayer run` uses.

It refuses a source of another dialect, naming that a later version adds other dialects, a field whose expr or filter holds a subquery, and a field or metric whose text is not one expression (see Expression text) as `invalid_layer`.

`--json` prints `{ ok: true, source, dialect, model, sql, params, columns: [{ name, ref, kind }], joins: [{ alias, via, model, type, for, through? }], row_filters: [{ model, alias, in, roles, rows }], session_time_zone, warnings?, as_of?, as_of_from? }`, where `as_of` and `as_of_from` (`clock` or `request`) are the last day of a request's `window` and where it came from (see Running totals and comparisons), `warnings`, when present, lists `{ code, message }` for each join the query adds that compiles with a warning (see Schema scope), and where each column's `kind` is `dimension`, `measure` or `metric`, each join names its alias, the declared join it follows as `model.join`, its target model, `left` or `inner`, `for` `request` or `definition`, for a metric, `part`, the model of the CTE that joins it, and for a bridge join's two joins `through`, `bridge` or `target` (see Bridges and multi-fact requests), and each row filter applied, in the SQL's order, names its model, the alias it limits, `in` `on` or `where`, the held roles whose filters limit it and `rows` `filtered` or `none` (for a metric, every part's in turn, each with `part`, the model of its CTE); or `{ ok: false, source, reason, refusals: [{ code, ref?, message, next?, reasons?, awaits? }], next }`, where `code` is one of `invalid_request`, `unknown_role`, `not_visible`, `unsupported_dialect`, `unsupported_metric`, `invalid_layer`, `no_join_path`, `ambiguous_join_path`, `join_fans_out`, `no_shared_dimension`, `scope.cross_schema_undeclared`, `metric.segments_on_metric`, `metric.default_time_missing`, `join.multi_fact_segments`, `join.through_second_path`, `join.through_filter_ungrouped`, `join.through_semi_additive`, `join.through_aggregate_unsupported` and `join.through_coarse_group`, a refusal's `next` says what resolves that refusal when it is specific to it, a `not_visible` refusal's `reasons` are the evaluator's reasons (`draft`, `protected_tag:<tag>`, `measure_hidden:<model.measure>`, ...) and `awaits` the approve targets it waits on when nothing but approval hides it, and the top-level `next` names the command to run next.

### Bridges and multi-fact requests

A bridge join (`through`; see the join in model files) declares that a model reaches a target by way of a bridge model, and carries the owner's dedupe rule: `distinct_pairs` (each (row, target value) pair counts once), `equal_split` (each of n target values gets 1/n of the figure) or `weight` (each gets its share of the numeric bridge column named by `through.weight`). A draft may leave the rule unset; an approved bridge join must have it. The join's `keys` pair the model's columns with the bridge's and `through.keys` pair the bridge's with the target's; the relationship is `one_to_many`, and the generic `model.join_one_to_many` warning is not raised for it.

`semlayer validate` checks each bridge join with five error codes: `join.through_bridge_unknown` (the bridge is not a third model of the source), `join.through_columns_missing` (a key or weight column is not in the snapshot's table), `join.through_dedupe_missing` (an approved join without the rule), `join.through_relationship_invalid` (a relationship other than `one_to_many`) and `join.through_weight_invalid` (a weight without dedupe `weight`, dedupe `weight` without a weight, or a weight column that is not numeric). The join makes two hops, model to bridge and bridge to target, and each obeys the schema-pair rule of Schema scope as a join of its own (`scope.cross_schema_undeclared`). The approval hash of a bridge join includes the bridge, its keys, the dedupe rule and the weight.

`semlayer check-join` measures a bridge join in two queries (`scripts/lib/sql/through-check.ts`): the hop from the model to the bridge, as any join (query `join:<model>.<join>`, its `to` and `keys` the join's own), and the bridge (query `through:<model>.<join>`), whose counts it writes under `evidence.through`: `model` and `keys` (the bridge and the bridge-to-target keys it measured), `fact_rows`, `bridge_rows`, `duplicate_rows` (bridge rows repeating a pair an earlier row holds), `rows_without_fact` and `rows_without_target` (bridge rows whose key matches no row; an empty key matches none), `facts_unbridged`, `facts_one_value` and `facts_many_values` (rows of the model with no bridge row, with one distinct target value, with several) and `max_values_per_fact`. They are counts only, never a value of a table. The plain join's match-rate and fan-out findings give way to the bridge's: rows with no bridge row and bridge rows that name nothing are warnings, repeated pairs a note. Each hop must pass the schema-pair guard; a bridge that is not a third model is refused as `join.through_bridge_unknown` (next `semlayer status`), a key column the bridge's or target's table lacks as `join.through_columns_missing` (next `semlayer tables --source <s>`), and under `--all` either is skipped with its reason. `semlayer validate` warns, `join.through_facts_unbridged`, while that evidence shows rows of the model with no bridge row: a figure grouped by the target shows them under an empty value (a `left` join) or leaves them out (`inner`). The evidence is outside the approval hash, so the warning is for a join that is not settled: a draft, one changed since its approval, or one whose current approval was not given on a readback that stated the stored count (the ledger's yes names the readback, and `.semlayer/pending` keeps what it showed). The owner's approval of the join on a readback that states the count settles it; `semlayer check-join` that changes the count asks again. The warning names `semlayer readback --targets <join>`.

The readback of a bridge join (`scripts/lib/readback-bridge.ts`) names the model, the bridge and the target with both key pairs, what happens to a row with no bridge row, the dedupe rule and what it does to a figure (`distinct_pairs`: a row with several target values adds its whole figure to each; `equal_split` and `weight`: its shares add up to its figure once), and says that totals by the target can add up to more than the overall total: under `distinct_pairs` they can, and under the other rules they add up to the overall total of the rows that have a target value. Then the measured counts of `evidence.through`. `semlayer readback` refuses a bridge join with no dedupe rule (`readback_blocked`, its `semlayer apply` input the `semlayer set <ref> through <value>` that adds the rule, the owner's choice in place of its placeholder) or with no bridge evidence, or evidence of another bridge or other keys (`readback_blocked`, next the one `semlayer check-join --source <s> --model <m> --join <j>` that measures it), so the owner never says yes to a bridge they were not shown.

A request whose measures come from several models, such as orders and refunds by month, is a multi-fact request. It compiles in parts as a metric does (see Reference compiler): one CTE per model, each its measures grouped by the request's dimensions, combined on those dimensions through `"request groups"`, the union of every part's groups, each part left joined to it, so a month only refunds have still appears, and no part's rows are joined to another's, so neither total is multiplied. A selected measure is its part's column as it is, null for a group its part has no rows for, never 0. Each part's model must reach every dimension and dimension filter of the request through one path of to-one joins or have a dimension of the same name; a dimension only one model reaches is refused, `no_shared_dimension`, naming the model, never cross joined. Without dimensions each part is one row and they are cross joined. A filter on a measure of one of the models goes in the outer `WHERE`. `model` in the output is the first measure's model.

- Access applies to the request as a whole and to each part: a measure or dimension the user may not see refuses the whole request as `not_visible`, never a partial answer, and each part applies the user's row filters to its own model. A protected field in either part protects its column of the result as in any request.
- A request's segments narrow the one model of its measures, and a multi-fact request has none, so a multi-fact request that names segments is refused, `join.multi_fact_segments`; a segment a measure names still applies inside that measure's part.
- A bare `:grain` field reads the default time dimension of the request's first measure, as in any request, and every other part groups by that dimension as the request would name it in full: through its path when its model reaches the first measure's model, else by its own time dimension of that name.

A request that groups by a field of a bridge join's target compiles through the bridge (`scripts/lib/compiler/bridge.ts`), on postgres as on duckdb. The base model reaches the target through a bridge join of its own straight to it, when no to-one path reaches it; a bridge join one hop away, or two bridge joins straight to it, are refused (`no_join_path`, `ambiguous_join_path`). The query adds two joins, both with the join's `type`: the bridge, as a subselect aliased `"<model>.<join> bridge"` of one row per pair of the bridge's columns in `keys` and `through.keys` (`group by` both, so a pair the bridge lists twice counts once), then the target, aliased `"<model>.<join>"` as a request path. Under `equal_split` the subselect gives each pair `"semlayer_share"`, `1.0 / count(*) over (partition by <the bridge's columns in keys>)`; under `weight` the pair's total weight over its row's total weight, or an equal share when that total is 0 or empty (so the row's shares still add up to its figure). A `sum` is then `sum((<expr>) * coalesce(<share>, 1))` and a `count` `coalesce(sum(coalesce(<share>, 1)), 0)` (for `count(<expr>)`, the share where the expr is not null), each with the measure's filter; a row with no bridge row has no share and counts in full. Under `distinct_pairs` every aggregate is written as without a bridge, over the pairs. So, grouped by the target:

- `distinct_pairs`: a row with several target values adds its whole figure to each of them, so totals by the target can add up to more than the overall total, which counts each row once; an `avg`, `min`, `max` or `count_distinct` is over the rows that have that value.
- `equal_split` and `weight`: a row's shares add up to its figure once, so totals by the target add up to the overall total of the rows that have a target value, to the database's rounding of the fractions. Only a `sum` or a `count` is shared; another aggregate is refused, `join.through_aggregate_unsupported`.
- A row with no bridge row shows under an empty target value with a `left` join and is left out with `inner`; so is the share of a bridge row whose target key matches no target row. A figure the request does not group by the target is compiled without the bridge, so it is the same with and without the bridge join declared.
- The target's key is taken as unique, as a `many_to_one` join's is. A grouping coarser than the target (a target field two target rows share) adds the shares of each pair in the group, which is right. Under `distinct_pairs` a computed target field is refused (`join.through_coarse_group`), since a row listed with two targets of one value would count twice in that group; a plain column is taken as one value per target row.
- A filter on a field the base reaches only through a bridge join is refused, `join.through_filter_ungrouped`, unless the request groups by that same field: without it the filter would count a row once for each of its target values that match. A measure filter, a segment or a row filter that reads the target through the bridge join is a join reference through a `one_to_many` join and refused as a layer error.
- A request through two bridge joins is refused, `join.through_second_path`; a semi-additive measure grouped through a bridge, `join.through_semi_additive`; and a bridge join with no dedupe rule, or with `weight` and no weight column, `invalid_layer`, naming `semlayer add-bridge --dedupe <rule>`.
- Access: the user's row filters on the bridge go in the subselect's `WHERE`, so the shares are taken over the pairs they see (listed in `row_filters` with `in` `on`), and theirs on the target in its join's `ON` clause. A bridge the user may not see refuses the request as `not_visible`, naming the bridge and its reasons, as does a target field hidden from them; a protected target field is masked in output as any field. Each of the two hops obeys the schema-pair rule (`scope.cross_schema_undeclared`, or its warning on an approved join).
- In a multi-fact request each part follows these rules, so one model's part may group through its bridge while another groups by its own field. A golden whose request groups through a bridge join reads back that join's dedupe sentences, "Grouped through the bridge join <model>.<join>: ...", among its request's words.

### Checking a rule

`semlayer check-rule --source <s> --model <m> (--filter <expr> | --dimension-expr <expr>)` answers whether the layer can express a rule on a model before anyone writes it into a field (`scripts/lib/compiler/check-rule.ts`). A rule is read as the compiler and `semlayer validate` read a measure filter or a dimension expr: the model's own columns (a dotted name on one reads a struct field) and join references of one or two `many_to_one` or `one_to_one` hops (see Join references), and nothing else. It is not expressible, with a `code`, a `reason` and a `suggestion` that names what to do next, when it:

- is not one SQL expression (`invalid_rule`): empty, with unbalanced parentheses, an unterminated string or comment, a block comment inside a block comment (Postgres and DuckDB nest them, other databases do not), a semicolon, a bound parameter or a `{{ }}` placeholder;
- has a subquery (`subquery`);
- holds an aggregate or window function, such as `count`, `sum`, `min`, `max`, `avg`, `first`, `row_number` or any call with `over`, quoted or after a dot as well (`aggregate`): a rule is read row by row, so the suggestion names the model aggregated over and the view to ask the owner for, "one row per `<grain>` and `<function>_<column>`", the grain being the model's own (from its `grain`, "One row per customer.") or, for a window, its `partition by` column (a join key's target model's grain when the column is the single key of a to-one join);
- names another table or schema, or calls a function by its schema (`other_table`), reads through a `one_to_many` join (`to_many_join`) or goes more than two hops (`too_many_hops`);
- names a column the snapshot's table lacks (`unknown_column`), on the model or on a join's target, naming up to three closest column or join names; a name that is a dimension of the model gets the dimension's expr as the suggestion;
- needs a join whose evidence shows fan-out (`join_fans_out`, as `semlayer sql` refuses it, naming `semlayer check-join`), or a join the layer gets wrong (`invalid_layer`);
- gives a count query that fails the read-only check `semlayer run` uses (`not_read_only`).

An expressible rule is measured by one read-only query over the model's table with the rule's joins, added as definition joins by the compiler's join planner (aliases as `semlayer sql` writes them): `count(*) filter (where <rule>) as rows_matched, count(*) as rows_total` on duckdb and postgres, and `coalesce(sum(case when <rule> then 1 else 0 end), 0)` for `rows_matched` on other dialects; a dimension expr counts the rows where it is not null, `count(<expr>)`. An `inner` join drops the rows it finds no match for from both counts. duckdb and postgres sources run it; any source takes `--emit-sql`, `--results <file>` and `--plan <plan_id>` (query id `rule:<model>:<hash>`, the hash the first 8 hex digits of the SHA-256 of its SQL, so a result stored for one rule answers no other; `max_rows` 2; see Query-plan protocol). It writes no file except the plan `--emit-sql` stores. When the query fails on a rule that reads a protected column, the database's message, which can quote a value, is not shown. The same rule and layer give the same SQL and plan id.

`--json` prints `{ source, model, kind, rule, expressible: true, joins_used, joins, sql, rows_matched, rows_total, next }`, where `kind` is `filter` or `dimension_expr`, `joins_used` lists the join chains the rule reads through in reading order (`customer`, `order.test_account`) and `joins` is as in `semlayer sql`; or `{ source, model, kind, rule, expressible: false, code, reason, suggestion }`, exiting 1. `next` is the `semlayer set` that puts the rule on a measure's `filter` or a dimension's `expr`, approved only after the owner's yes to its readback.

## Schema exports

`references/exports/<dialect>.sql` holds one catalog query per database: `postgres`, `redshift`, `mysql`, `sqlserver`, `snowflake`, `bigquery`, `databricks` and `duckdb`. `semlayer export-sql --dialect <d>` prints it. The `mysql`, `sqlserver` and `redshift` exports are kept but unsupported, as is every dialect but `postgres` and `duckdb` for new work (`references/other-dialects.md`). Each query is read-only and reads only the catalog, never table data. It leaves out the system schemas, and its header comment says how to run it, how to save the result as CSV (tab-separated from `mysql --batch`, which `semlayer import` reads as well), and how to restrict it to some schemas: uncomment the filter line marked `semlayer:schema-filter` and list them, or let `semlayer export-sql --schemas` apply it. BigQuery reads each dataset's metadata separately: its blocks between `semlayer:per-dataset` and `semlayer:end-per-dataset` read `my_dataset`, and `--schemas` repeats each block once per dataset, joined by `union all`. It returns one row per column, with exactly these lowercase columns:

| Column | Holds |
|---|---|
| `table_schema` | the schema (a BigQuery dataset) |
| `table_name` | the table or view |
| `table_type` | the catalog's table type, such as `BASE TABLE` or `VIEW` |
| `table_comment` | the table's comment, or null |
| `row_count` | the catalog's row estimate, or null |
| `column_name` | the column |
| `ordinal_position` | the column's position in the table, from 1 |
| `data_type` | the column's type as the catalog writes it |
| `is_nullable` | whether the column may be null |
| `column_comment` | the column's comment, or null |
| `is_primary_key` | `true` or `false` |
| `pk_ordinal` | the column's position in the primary key, or null |
| `fk_name` | the name of a foreign key the column is in, or null |
| `fk_ordinal` | the column's position in that foreign key, or null |
| `references_schema`, `references_table`, `references_column` | the column that foreign key references, or null |

The five foreign-key columns are null when the column is in no foreign key. A column in several foreign keys appears once per key, so its other values repeat on each of those rows.

`semlayer import` reads these columns when present, as an `information_schema` import: `primary_key` from the columns whose `is_primary_key` is true, ordered by `pk_ordinal` (else by `ordinal_position`); `foreign_keys` grouped by `fk_name` (else by `references_schema` and `references_table`), each key's columns ordered by `fk_ordinal`; `table_comment` as the table's `description`; and `row_count`. An export without them imports as an information_schema export always has: tables, columns, types, comments and row counts where present.

### Table filters

`semlayer export-sql --include <p>... --exclude <p>...` takes patterns in the Scope patterns grammar and writes them into the query at its line marked `semlayer:table-filter`, as a predicate on the catalog's schema and table names: a table is kept when it matches an include (or no include is given) and no exclude, exclude winning. The filter adds predicates and nothing else, so the query still reads only the catalog. Each pattern value is a SQL string literal escaped for the dialect (a quote doubled or backslash-escaped, a backslash doubled where the dialect's literals read it as an escape, an `E''` string on Postgres), never spliced in raw, and on MySQL and Databricks, where a setting decides whether a literal reads a backslash as an escape (`NO_BACKSLASH_ESCAPES`, `spark.sql.parser.escapedStringLiterals`), a glob holding a backslash is not written into the query; a pattern holding a line break or control character is refused, since the header comment lists it. A pattern is written into the query only when the SQL means exactly what the grammar means:

- An exact name becomes `IN` and a glob `LIKE` (`*` as `%`, `?` as `_`), on the table name, or on `schema || '.' || table` (`concat` on MySQL and SQL Server) when the pattern is qualified. `%`, `_` and the escape character (and `[` on SQL Server) are escaped with `!` and `escape '!'`; BigQuery's `LIKE` takes no `ESCAPE` clause and escapes with a backslash. MySQL compares through `convert(... using utf8mb4) collate utf8mb4_bin` and SQL Server through `collate Latin1_General_100_BIN2`, so their default case- and accent-insensitive collations do not widen a match, and both use `LIKE` for an exact name too, since their `=` ignores trailing spaces.
- A letter in a quoted part is compared as written. When every case-insensitive letter is ASCII and no letter is quoted, the name is compared as `lower(name)` with the pattern lowercased: on Postgres (as `lower(name collate "C")`, which changes A-Z only), and on DuckDB and MySQL when the pattern also has no case-insensitive `i` or `k` (their `lower()` also makes `i` from `İ` and `k` from the Kelvin sign). Any other case-insensitive letter is written as the class of every character the grammar matches for it (`[fF]`; `[Σςσ]` for `σ`, `[µΜμ]` for `μ`): in a `LIKE` class on SQL Server, and in a case-sensitive regex anchored at both ends on Postgres and Redshift (`~`), Snowflake (`regexp_like`), BigQuery (`regexp_contains`), Databricks (`rlike`) and DuckDB (`regexp_matches`). MySQL has neither, so it does not write such a pattern.
- A `re:` pattern becomes the same case-sensitive regex test, rewritten into a form all of those dialects read as the grammar's JavaScript regex: each letter as the class of its two cases and a class's letters with their other case added (the dialects' case-insensitive flags also fold characters outside ASCII, the Kelvin sign as `k`, and the grammar does not), `\d` as `[0-9]`, `\w` as `[A-Za-z0-9_]`, an escaped metacharacter as a one-character class, `(?:` as `(`, a lazy repeat as a greedy one. A regex using anything else (`\s`, `\b`, a backreference, a lookaround or named group, an empty alternative, a repeat count over 255, a character outside ASCII, a literal `[`, `]`, `\` or `^`) is not written into the query.
- MySQL's `REGEXP` follows the name column's collation, which varies with `lower_case_table_names`, and its regex flavour differs between MySQL and MariaDB versions, so a `re:` pattern is not written into a MySQL query; SQL Server has no regular expressions.
- Two kinds of name can still be matched differently by a written pattern. A one-character wildcard (`?`, or `.` and a negated class in a regex) matches one character in SQL and one UTF-16 code unit in the grammar, so a name holding a character outside the Basic Multilingual Plane, such as an emoji, can differ; and the dialects' regexes read a line break differently from JavaScript's (`.` matches it on Postgres), so a name holding one can differ where the pattern became a regex (Databricks' `$` also matches before a final line break).

A pattern the query cannot hold is deferred to `semlayer import` (the export then reads more table definitions, still never table data). Includes match together, so one deferred include defers every include; an exclude is deferred alone. The query's header comment lists the patterns it applies and the deferred ones with the reason for each, and names the `semlayer import --source <source> --file <file>` with the `--include` and `--exclude` that apply them; the run hint on stderr carries the same import command, and a second stderr line says how many patterns were deferred. `--json` adds `filters: { pushed: [{ kind, pattern, method }], deferred: [{ kind, pattern, reason }], import_command }`, `method` one of `in`, `like`, `regex` and `import_command` null when nothing is deferred; without `--include` or `--exclude` the output has no `filters`.

## dbt semantic import

`semlayer import --source <s> --file target/manifest.json --semantic` also reads the manifest's `semantic_models` and `metrics` and reports them. It reads only: the snapshot is saved as for any import, and no file of the layer is written. The reader (`lib/importers/dbt-semantic.ts`) is a pure function of the parsed manifest, with no file, database or clock. Names, descriptions, exprs and filters in a manifest are untrusted text: they are carried as data and never run. `--semantic` on a file that is not a dbt manifest is refused with code `semantic_needs_dbt_manifest` and the command to run instead.

| Read | Holds |
|---|---|
